10-source RAG agent benchmark (Copilot Studio)

Both agents now have 10 knowledge sources (about 3 GB of data). 22 user queries x 5 runs each, one new conversation per query. Driver: Copilot Studio Agents SDK (CopilotClient). Run on 2026-10-06, both benchmarks in parallel from the same machine. Click an answer to expand it. Yellow rows are over 30s, red rows are empty or fallback answers.

  • AI Search is about 1.9x faster at p50. The whole gap is the knowledge-search phase: SharePoint search is 10.2s slower at p50, versus a 10.0s total gap (AI Search generation is about 1s slower, which offsets some of it).
  • AI Search is much more predictable: within-question std dev 0.6s versus 4.9s for SharePoint, and its slowest conversation took 15s versus 120s.
  • Configuration: PWAISearchAgent had 'Allow ungrounded responses' turned OFF and published (2026-10-05 17:05 UTC) before this run, unlike the 2026-10-05 benchmark. PWSharepointAgent has model knowledge off.
  • Phase definitions: plan is the time to the first plan event; search runs from the first search step to the first answer token; generation is the remainder. Search steps counted from DynamicPlanStepFinished events.
  • Answer health: PWSharepointAgent: 0 empty/fallback answers out of 110; PWAISearchAgent: 0 empty/fallback answers out of 110.

Benchmark notes, setup and references

  • 1. Benchmark design. Two Copilot Studio agents are compared: PWSharepointAgent (10 SharePoint knowledge sources, searched by Copilot Studio's built-in SharePoint knowledge) and PWAISearchAgent (10 Azure AI Search knowledge sources, one index per batch). 22 queries x 5 runs per agent, one new conversation per query, latency measured end to end. Knowledge sources overview | SharePoint as knowledge | Azure AI Search as knowledge
  • 2. Benchmark scripts. Python, using the Microsoft 365 Agents SDK Copilot Studio client (microsoft-agents-copilotstudio-client: CopilotClient, start_conversation, ask_question). Every streamed activity is timestamped on arrival, so plan, search and generation phases are derived from the plan events. The earlier benchmark used the REST endpoint behind the same client. Integrate with the Microsoft 365 Agents SDK | Python client library | Agent debugger (performance timeline) | Application Insights
  • 3. Dataset. About 1,000 PDF files (10 batches of about 100), each a research paper, about 3 GB in total. The same files back both agents: 10 SharePoint folders, and 10 Azure AI Search indexes (about 42,900 chunks, 1.9 GB of index). Unstructured data as knowledge
  • 4. SharePoint indexer permissions. The Azure AI Search SharePoint Online indexer (preview) reads with Microsoft Graph application permissions Files.Read.All and Sites.Read.All (admin consent required), or Sites.Selected plus Files.Read.All to limit it to chosen sites. Delegated equivalents exist for the delegated-auth option. SharePoint Online indexer
  • 5. Adding a SharePoint indexer to Azure AI Search. Register an Entra app and grant the permissions above, create a SharePoint data source, create the index and an indexer, then run it and monitor status. Follow the doc rather than a copied step list, since the feature is in preview and changes. Index data from SharePoint Online
  • 6. Results (total latency). PWSharepointAgent p50 21.1s, p90 26.5s, p95 31.8s. PWAISearchAgent p50 11.1s, p90 12.0s, p95 12.7s.
  • 7. Private endpoint for Azure AI Search. Copilot Studio reaches a private AI Search through the AI Search connector running in a Power Platform environment that is VNet-integrated (delegated subnet), with the search service behind a private endpoint in the same VNet. Reference architecture and security notes: Copilot Studio with Azure AI Search: security considerations | Power Platform virtual network support | Private endpoint for Azure AI Search
  • 8. Phase definitions. Plan: time to the first plan event. Search: first search step to the first answer text. Generation: the remainder. Percentiles use nearest-rank on 110 samples per agent.
  • 9. Caveats. Both benchmarks ran in parallel from one machine. Answer quality was spot-checked, not scored. Results depend on region, tenant load and agent configuration at the time of the run.

11retrieval augmented generation for large language models16:45:4124.26.017.21.111388yes
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is an approach that enhances largRetrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is an approach that enhances large language models (LLMs) by retrieving relevant information from external knowledge sources and supplying it to the model before generating a response. In a typical RAG workflow: - A user submits a query. - A retrieval system searches documents, databases, or knowledge repositories for relevant content. - The retrieved content is provided to the LLM as context. - The LLM generates an answer grounded in that retrieved information. Benefits of RAG include: - Access to up-to-date information without retraining the model. - Reduced hallucinations by grounding responses in retrieved sources. - Ability to use private or domain-specific knowledge. - Improved performance on question-answering tasks and enterprise search applications. The concept is closely related to retrieval-based question answering, where benchmarks such as ARC include questions designed to challenge retrieval-based algorithms. [1] Common RAG components are: - Document store (knowledge base) - Embedding model - Vector database/search engine - Retriever - LLM generator RAG is widely used in enterprise chatbots, document assistants, customer support systems, and knowledge management applications. [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf"

[1] - Evaluating truthfulness and reasoning performance on dedicated benchmarks to monitor hallucination-related behavior. 13efficient fine tuning with low rank adaptation16:46:3123.74.417.31.911141yes
Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniLow-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique for large language models. Instead of updating all model weights, LoRA freezes the original model and trains small low-rank matrices that are injected into selected layers. This greatly reduces the number of trainable parameters, memory usage, and training cost. A cited example describes parameter-efficient tuning using LoRA with: - Rank (r) = 64 - Alpha (α) = 128 - No dropout - INT4 quantization - Targeting all linear modules during fine-tuning [1] The same source notes that QLoRA was used to fine-tune an 8B financial language model efficiently on 8 A100 GPUs while training only a small subset of parameters. [1] In practice, LoRA is popular because it: - Reduces GPU memory requirements - Speeds up fine-tuning - Preserves the original model weights - Enables task-specific adapters that can be swapped in and out easily QLoRA extends LoRA by combining it with quantized base models for even greater efficiency. [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2408.11878v2.pdf "2408.11878v2.pdf"

16long context understanding and attention mechanisms16:47:3524.94.318.81.711573yes
**Long Context Understanding** Long context understanding refers to a**Long Context Understanding** Long context understanding refers to a model’s ability to process and reason over very long sequences while preserving relevant information and relationships across distant parts of the input. Long-context systems use techniques such as Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), Sliding Window Attention (SWA), and rolling buffer caches to efficiently handle long sequences while reducing memory and computation costs. For example, SWA can maintain dependencies beyond the immediate window and support attention spans exceeding 14,000 tokens. [1] **Attention Mechanisms** Attention mechanisms allow a model to determine which parts of the input are most relevant when processing a token. In transformers, each input is transformed into Query (Q), Key (K), and Value (V) vectors. Attention scores are computed from Q and K, normalized with Softmax, and used to weight the Value vectors, producing context-aware representations. Multi-head attention extends this by learning multiple attention patterns simultaneously. [2] Research also shows that sparse, focused attention can improve learning efficiency and reasoning performance. [3] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2402.16901v2.pdf "2402.16901v2.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2410.05459v2.pdf "2410.05459v2.pdf"
17knowledge distillation for smaller language models16:48:0123.84.318.11.511274yes
Knowledge distillation is a technique where a large “teacher” model trKnowledge distillation is a technique where a large “teacher” model transfers its learned behavior to a smaller “student” model, allowing the student to achieve much of the teacher’s performance with fewer parameters and lower inference cost. The process typically follows a teacher–student architecture and can transfer knowledge through output responses (response-based distillation) or intermediate representations/features (feature-based distillation). [1] For smaller language models, distillation is often used to: - Reduce model size and memory requirements. - Improve inference speed. - Preserve reasoning and language capabilities from larger models. [1] An example from the knowledge base describes using chain-of-thought reasoning data distilled from a larger model (DeepSeek R1) to improve a 7B model’s reasoning capabilities before reinforcement learning fine-tuning. [2] In practice, distillation helps smaller LLMs remain competitive while being easier and cheaper to deploy. [2] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2405.01558v3.pdf "2405.01558v3.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf"
18quantization techniques for efficient model inference16:48:2618.04.013.70.211194yes
Quantization reduces model size and speeds inference by representing wQuantization reduces model size and speeds inference by representing weights and activations with lower-precision numbers instead of full-precision formats. Common techniques include: - Post-Training Quantization (PTQ): Converts a trained model to lower precision (e.g., INT8) without retraining. - Quantization-Aware Training (QAT): Simulates quantization during training to preserve accuracy after deployment. - Dynamic Quantization: Quantizes weights ahead of time and activations during inference. - Static Quantization: Quantizes both weights and activations using calibration data. - Mixed-Precision Quantization: Uses different precisions (e.g., INT4, INT8, FP16) for different layers. - Weight-Only Quantization: Quantizes weights while keeping activations at higher precision. An example from the knowledge base is Reverb Turbo, which provides an INT8-quantized ASR model optimized for speed and memory-sensitive applications. [1] Benefits include reduced memory usage, lower latency, and improved throughput, often with minimal accuracy loss. [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2410.03930v3.pdf "2410.03930v3.pdf"

It combines human feedback with reinforcement learning so a model learns to produce responses that people judge as more helpful, accurate, or appropriate. [1] The knowledge base also notes that post-training methods for LLMs include “reinforcement learning from human feedback” and that modern reasoning models often use reinforcement learning techniques to improve capabilities after supervised fine-tuning. 114mixture of experts architectures and routing16:50:28120.04.2113.62.211180yes
The knowledge base contains only limited references to Mixture of ExpeThe knowledge base contains only limited references to Mixture of Experts (MoE), not a detailed explanation. It notes that MoE training has been used to improve model efficiency and performance, with examples including Mixtral and GLaM. MoE is described as an efficiency-enhancing technique that has achieved significant success in language models. [1] In general terms, a Mixture of Experts architecture consists of: - Multiple specialized neural subnetworks called experts. - A routing mechanism that decides which expert(s) process each input token. - Sparse activation, where only a small subset of experts is used per token, reducing computation. Routing methods typically include: - Top-1 routing: send a token to the highest-scoring expert. - Top-k routing: send a token to the k highest-scoring experts. - Load-balancing mechanisms: encourage traffic to be distributed across experts. The knowledge base specifically cites Mixtral as a “Mixture of Experts” model and references GLaM as an efficient scaling approach using MoE. 118instruction tuning and preference optimization16:53:3323.74.017.91.811104yes
**Instruction tuning** is a post-training method where a pre-trained m**Instruction tuning** is a post-training method where a pre-trained model is fine-tuned on datasets of prompts and desired responses so it learns to follow instructions and perform tasks more effectively. Instruction-tuned models are trained on large instruction datasets and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) examples. [1]​[2] **Preference optimization** is a post-training approach that trains models using preference data, where preferred responses are compared against rejected responses. One example is Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), which fine-tunes a model using preference pairs to better align outputs with desired behavior. [1] In practice, many LLM pipelines apply: 1. Supervised Fine-Tuning (instruction tuning) 2. Preference Optimization (e.g., DPO) to improve instruction following and response quality. 119inference time scaling and chain of thought reasoning16:53:5824.83.919.02.011456yes
Inference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s reasoning peInference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s reasoning performance by allocating more computation during inference (test time), rather than only making the model larger or training it longer. One approach is to increase the length of the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning process, allowing the model to spend more tokens and steps working through a problem. This has been reported to improve performance on tasks such as mathematics, coding, and scientific reasoning. [1] Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning is a technique where a model breaks a complex problem into smaller, intermediate reasoning steps. Research describes CoT as improving reasoning, generalization, and learning efficiency by introducing step-by-step computations and sparse sequential dependencies that make problems easier for transformers to learn. In parity-learning experiments, models trained with CoT required polynomial or near-linear samples, while models without CoT required exponentially more samples. [2] In short: - Chain-of-Thought = reasoning through intermediate steps. - Inference-time scaling = spending more compute at inference, often by generating longer reasoning chains. [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2410.05459v2.pdf "2410.05459v2.pdf"
120data quality and deduplication for model pretraining16:54:2418.44.111.62.711139yes
For model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical becFor model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical because duplicated or low-quality content can reduce training efficiency, increase memorization, and hurt downstream performance. Key practices include: - Removing low-quality documents (e.g., very short or metadata-only content). SlimPajama filters documents with fewer than 200 meaningful characters before training. [1] - Exact and near-duplicate removal using techniques such as MinHash, Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH), Bloom filters, and Jaccard similarity thresholds. [1] - Cross-source deduplication, not just within a single dataset, to eliminate repeated content across corpora. SlimPajama performs deduplication both within and across sources. [1] - Quality filtering with learned models. DCLM-BASELINE uses fastText and ELI5-based quality scores to retain only the highest-quality documents. [1] Reported outcomes include improved training efficiency, reduced memorization, and better downstream model performance. The model is Moxin-7B. In its architecture section, the authors state The model is Moxin-7B. In its architecture section, the authors state that they “extend the Mistral model architecture” and that while the original Mistral has 32 blocks, their extended model uses 36 transformer blocks. [1] Its pre-training is described as a three-phase process: - Phase 1 — Base Pre-training: - Trained with a fixed context length of 2,000 tokens. - Purpose: establish foundational language-modeling abilities, including syntax and basic semantic patterns. [1] - Phase 2 — Extended Context Pre-training: - Context length increased to 4,000 tokens. - Purpose: enable the model to capture longer dependencies and richer context. [1] - Phase 3 — Capability Enhancement: - Incorporates capability-specific enhancement data collected to improve abilities such as reasoning, mathematical problem-solving, and knowledge memorization. - The paper notes that this phase uses the capability-enhancement data described earlier and evaluates its contribution through ablations comparing Phases 1–2 against all three phases. 21retrieval augmented generation for large language models16:55:3131.84.127.40.311470yes
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is an approach that combines a laRetrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is an approach that combines a large language model (LLM) with an external knowledge retrieval system. Instead of relying only on information learned during training, the model retrieves relevant documents or data at query time and uses that information to generate responses. Typical RAG workflow: - A user submits a question. - A retrieval component searches a knowledge base for relevant content. - The retrieved passages are added to the model’s context. - The LLM generates an answer grounded in the retrieved information. Benefits: - Access to up-to-date information without retraining. - Reduced hallucinations by grounding responses in source material. - Ability to use private or domain-specific knowledge. - Improved performance on question-answering and enterprise search tasks. RAG is widely used in chatbots, enterprise knowledge assistants, document search systems, and AI agents that retrieve real-time information through tools and external data sources. The concept is closely related to retrieval-based question answering systems and agent workflows that combine LLMs with external tools and information retrieval capabilities. 23efficient fine tuning with low rank adaptation16:56:4319.14.014.50.61993yes
Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning methodLow-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method for large language models. Instead of updating all model weights, LoRA freezes the original model and trains small low-rank matrices that are injected into selected layers. This greatly reduces trainable parameters, memory usage, and training cost while maintaining strong performance. A related approach, QLoRA, combines LoRA with quantization. In one example, a financial LLM was instruction-tuned using QLoRA with INT4 quantization and LoRA settings of rank r=64 and α=128, enabling efficient fine-tuning on limited hardware. [1] Benefits of LoRA: - Lower GPU memory requirements - Faster training - Smaller adapter checkpoints - Easy task-specific customization LoRA is widely used for adapting foundation models to domain-specific tasks without retraining the full model. 24evaluating reasoning capabilities of large language models16:57:0321.94.217.00.711169yes
**Evaluating Reasoning Capabilities of Large Language Models** Reason**Evaluating Reasoning Capabilities of Large Language Models** Reasoning in LLMs is commonly evaluated using benchmark tasks that test logical deduction, mathematical problem-solving, commonsense reasoning, and multi-step inference. Common benchmarks include ARC (AI2 Reasoning Challenge), HellaSwag, MMLU, Winogrande, and PIQA. These datasets assess different aspects of reasoning, from scientific question answering to commonsense and physical reasoning. [1] Recent research also evaluates reasoning through Chain-of-Thought (CoT) methods. One study found that CoT can substantially improve learning efficiency by introducing sparse sequential dependencies and more interpretable attention patterns, leading to better performance on reasoning tasks. [2] Additional reasoning evaluations often use mathematics-focused benchmarks such as MATH-500, AMC, Minerva Math, and OlympiadBench. 25multimodal learning combining vision and language16:57:2717.44.011.71.711176yes
Multimodal learning that combines vision and language trains AI systemMultimodal learning that combines vision and language trains AI systems to understand and reason across both visual inputs (images, videos) and text. The goal is to learn a shared representation so that visual and textual information can be aligned and used together. For example, models can answer questions about images, generate captions, interpret charts, or perform cross-modal generation tasks. [1] Common approaches use a vision encoder (for images) and a language model (for text), then align their representations through multimodal training. Examples include systems that use CLIP as a visual encoder and a language model as a decoder, with multimodal instruction tuning to improve image-text understanding and reasoning. [2] Applications include image captioning, visual question answering, chart and table understanding, image retrieval, and cross-modal audio-video generation. 26long context understanding and attention mechanisms16:57:4524.54.117.92.411670yes
**Long Context Understanding and Attention Mechanisms** Attention mec**Long Context Understanding and Attention Mechanisms** Attention mechanisms are the core component that allow transformer models to understand relationships between tokens across a sequence. In self-attention, each token generates query, key, and value representations, and attention scores determine which parts of the context are most relevant. [1] For long-context processing, models often use specialized attention strategies to reduce memory and computation costs: - **Grouped-Query Attention (GQA):** Multiple query heads share key/value heads, reducing memory usage and improving decoding throughput. [2] - **Sliding Window Attention (SWA):** Attention is restricted to a moving window, enabling efficient processing of long sequences while preserving long-range dependencies. A window size of 4096 can provide a theoretical attention span exceeding 14,000 tokens. [2] - **Rolling Buffer Cache:** Stores keys and values within a fixed attention span, reducing inference memory usage. On 32K-token sequences, this approach can reduce cache memory by about 8×. [2] Research also shows that sparse and interpretable attention patterns can improve learning efficiency, particularly when reasoning is broken into step-by-step chains of thought. [3] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2402.16901v2.pdf "2402.16901v2.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2410.05459v2.pdf "2410.05459v2.pdf"
27knowledge distillation for smaller language models16:58:1118.83.613.81.411067yes
Knowledge distillation is a technique where a large “teacher” model trKnowledge distillation is a technique where a large “teacher” model transfers its knowledge to a smaller “student” model, enabling the student to achieve strong performance with fewer parameters and lower inference cost. It typically uses a teacher-student architecture and can transfer knowledge through output predictions (response-based distillation) or intermediate representations (feature-based distillation). [1] For smaller language models, distillation helps create compact and efficient models while retaining much of the teacher model’s capabilities. Research has also used reasoning traces generated by stronger models to improve smaller models’ reasoning abilities. For example, reasoning datasets were created from DeepSeek R1 outputs and used to train smaller 7B models. 28quantization techniques for efficient model inference16:58:3122.54.218.20.111175yes
Common quantization techniques for efficient model inference include: Common quantization techniques for efficient model inference include: - Post-Training Quantization (PTQ): Converts a trained model from floating-point (FP32) to lower precision (INT8, INT4) without retraining, reducing memory and improving inference speed. - Quantization-Aware Training (QAT): Simulates quantization effects during training to preserve accuracy after deployment. - Dynamic Quantization: Quantizes weights ahead of time and activations during inference. - Static Quantization: Quantizes both weights and activations using calibration data. - Mixed-Precision Quantization: Uses different precisions for different layers to balance speed and accuracy. - Weight-Only Quantization: Quantizes only model weights, commonly used for LLMs. - Low-Bit Quantization (INT8, INT4): Aggressively reduces model size and memory usage. One example in the knowledge base uses INT4 quantization with QLoRA for efficient fine-tuning. [1] These techniques typically reduce memory footprint, increase throughput, and lower deployment costs. [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2408.11878v2.pdf "2408.11878v2.pdf"

210synthetic data generation for training language models16:59:1218.04.011.62.411649yes
Synthetic data generation for training language models involves creatiSynthetic data generation for training language models involves creating artificial training examples instead of relying solely on human-authored data. The retrieved materials describe several uses of synthetic data in LLM training: - Reasoning datasets can be generated by a strong model and used to train smaller models. For example, OpenThoughts contains 114k synthetic reasoning examples, and OpenR1-Math-220k contains 220k math problems with reasoning traces generated from DeepSeek R1. [1] - Chain-of-thought (CoT) synthetic data can improve learning efficiency by introducing intermediate reasoning steps. Research showed transformers trained with CoT data required far fewer samples and learned more effectively than training without CoT. [2] - Multimodal synthetic instruction datasets are also used. FinLLaVA incorporates ALLaVA-4V, which uses GPT-4V–synthesized data for vision-language training. [3] - Modern post-training pipelines increasingly leverage synthetic data alongside instruction tuning and reinforcement learning to enhance reasoning capabilities. [1] Overall, synthetic data is used to expand high-quality training corpora, improve reasoning skills, and reduce dependence on expensive human annotation. 212tool use and planning in autonomous AI agents17:00:0317.94.311.91.711232yes
Tool use and planning are foundational capabilities in autonomous AI aTool use and planning are foundational capabilities in autonomous AI agents. Tool use enables an agent to interact with external systems, APIs, databases, or software beyond text generation. Function calls allow an LLM to recognize predefined operations, retrieve real-time information, and perform actions in external systems. When equipped with these capabilities, LLMs are often referred to as LLM-based agents. [1] Planning refers to organizing tasks into workflows. AI agent workflows connect tools, APIs, and LLMs in sequential or parallel steps to handle complex, multi-stage tasks. Compared with traditional workflows, agent-powered systems can process unstructured data, make flexible judgments, and adaptively link steps based on natural language reasoning. [1] A practical example is the StarWhisper Telescope system, where a central agent orchestrates multiple workflows such as observation planning, weather monitoring, telescope control, data processing, and reporting. The agent dynamically selects tools and workflows based on user requests and environmental conditions. 218instruction tuning and preference optimization17:02:1419.15.512.11.511031yes
**Instruction Tuning** is a post-training technique where a pretrained**Instruction Tuning** is a post-training technique where a pretrained model is fine-tuned on instruction-response datasets so it learns to follow user requests, answer questions, and perform tasks consistently. Datasets such as Tülu 3 and Infinity Instruct are examples used for supervised fine-tuning (SFT). [1] **Preference Optimization** further aligns model behavior with preferred responses. A common approach is Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), which trains on pairs of preferred and rejected answers created through preference annotation. The Tülu 3 preference pipeline uses prompt selection, response generation, and preference labeling to create these training pairs. [1] In practice, models are often trained with: 1. SFT (instruction tuning) 2. DPO or other preference optimization methods 3. Reinforcement-learning-based reasoning enhancement for advanced capabilities [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf"
219inference time scaling and chain of thought reasoning17:02:3418.64.112.81.811292yes
Inference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s reasoning peInference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s reasoning performance by allocating more computation during inference, often by generating longer reasoning processes rather than only increasing model size or training data. One example cited in the knowledge base is OpenAI’s o1 series, which improves reasoning by increasing the length of the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning process. This approach has shown gains in mathematics, coding, and scientific reasoning tasks. [1] Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning is a technique where a model breaks a complex problem into smaller, intermediate reasoning steps. Research in the knowledge base states that CoT enhances reasoning, improves generalization, and can make learning more sample-efficient by introducing sparse sequential dependencies that lead to more interpretable attention patterns. [2] In short: - CoT = step-by-step reasoning process. [2] - Inference-time scaling = spending more compute at inference time, often by extending CoT reasoning. [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2410.05459v2.pdf "2410.05459v2.pdf"
220data quality and deduplication for model pretraining17:02:5420.14.112.83.311338yes
For model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical becFor model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical because duplicate or low-quality data can reduce training efficiency, increase memorization, and hurt generalization. Key practices found in the knowledge base include: - Remove low-quality content before training. SlimPajama filters out very short or metadata-only documents to improve corpus quality. [1] - Perform exact and near-duplicate removal using MinHash, Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH), Jaccard similarity, suffix-array pipelines, and Bloom filters. [1] - Deduplicate both within and across data sources, not just within a single source. SlimPajama removed duplicates across sources and pruned 49.6% of RedPajama data. [1] - Apply quality filtering using learned classifiers. DCLM-BASELINE uses fastText and ELI5-based scoring to retain only the highest-quality documents. [1] - For code data, near-deduplication can identify large amounts of redundant content; one example removed about 40% of permissively licensed code files after MinHash/LSH clustering and Jaccard similarity checks. [1] These methods are reported to improve training efficiency, reduce memorization, and improve downstream model performance. In its architecture section, the authors state that they extend the Mistral architecture and increase it from 32 blocks to 36 transformer blocks: “While the original Mistral model comprises 32 blocks, our extended model uses 36 transformer blocks.” [1] Its pre-training is described as having three phases: • Phase 1 – Base Pre-training: - Trained with a fixed context length of 2,000 tokens. - Purpose: establish foundational language-modeling abilities and learn syntax and basic semantic patterns. 31retrieval augmented generation for large language models17:04:0739.03.818.316.91944yes
**Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) for Large Language Models** - **Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) for Large Language Models** - RAG combines large language models (LLMs) with external retrieval systems to enhance their responses by incorporating relevant information from documents or databases. - The process involves retrieving pertinent data based on the user's query and then generating an answer using both the retrieved content and the LLM's internal knowledge. - This approach improves accuracy, factuality, and domain-specific performance, especially in areas where LLMs may lack up-to-date or specialized information. - RAG is particularly useful for tasks requiring real-time data, domain expertise, or multimodal inputs, and is widely adopted in financial, legal, and scientific applications to bridge knowledge gaps and support complex reasoning [1]. 33efficient fine tuning with low rank adaptation17:05:1132.83.727.91.211161yes
Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniLow-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique for large language models. Instead of updating all model weights, LoRA freezes the original model and trains small low-rank matrices that are injected into selected layers. This greatly reduces the number of trainable parameters, memory usage, and training cost while preserving most of the model's performance. A cited example of efficient fine-tuning uses QLoRA with LoRA parameters r=64 and α=128, targeting all linear modules while using INT4 quantization for further efficiency. This approach enables instruction tuning with significantly reduced computational requirements. [1] The same source describes LoRA as a parameter-efficient tuning method applied during instruction fine-tuning of an 8B-parameter financial language model. [1] Benefits: - Lower GPU memory requirements - Faster training - Smaller storage footprint for task-specific adapters - Ability to maintain a single base model with multiple adapters for different tasks [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2408.11878v2.pdf "2408.11878v2.pdf"

36long context understanding and attention mechanisms17:06:2916.64.311.50.911175yes
Long-context understanding refers to a model’s ability to process and Long-context understanding refers to a model’s ability to process and reason over very long sequences while maintaining relevant information from earlier parts of the input. Techniques such as Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), Sliding Window Attention (SWA), and rolling buffer caches are used to support long contexts efficiently while reducing memory and computation costs. For example, SWA can preserve dependencies beyond the immediate context window and support attention spans exceeding 14,000 tokens. [1] Attention mechanisms allow a model to determine which parts of an input sequence are most relevant when processing a token. In transformers, inputs are transformed into query (Q), key (K), and value (V) vectors. Attention scores are computed from Q and K, normalized with Softmax, and used to weight V vectors, enabling the model to focus on important information across the sequence. 37knowledge distillation for smaller language models17:06:4622.74.516.12.111294yes
**Knowledge Distillation (KD)** is a technique that transfers knowledg**Knowledge Distillation (KD)** is a technique that transfers knowledge from a larger, more capable "teacher" model to a smaller "student" model, enabling the student to achieve strong performance with fewer parameters and lower inference cost. KD typically follows a teacher–student architecture where the teacher guides the student's learning through output responses or intermediate representations. [1] For smaller language models, distillation can: - Reduce model size while preserving much of the teacher's capability. - Improve deployment efficiency on resource-constrained hardware. - Transfer reasoning behaviors and specialized skills from larger models. [1] An example from LLM research is Moxin-7B, which improved reasoning by using chain-of-thought data distilled from DeepSeek R1 before reinforcement-learning-based refinement. [2] Common KD approaches include: - Response-based distillation: matching teacher outputs/logits. - Feature-based distillation: matching internal layer representations. 38quantization techniques for efficient model inference17:07:1021.04.115.81.211191yes
Common quantization approaches for efficient inference include: - PosCommon quantization approaches for efficient inference include: - Post-Training Quantization (PTQ): Converts trained model weights and activations from higher precision (e.g., FP32) to lower precision formats without retraining. - Quantization-Aware Training (QAT): Simulates low-precision arithmetic during training to preserve accuracy after quantization. - Integer Quantization: Uses INT8 or INT4 representations to reduce memory and computation costs. An example in the knowledge base reports parameter-efficient tuning with INT4 quantization. [1] - Mixed-Precision Inference: Combines lower-precision formats such as FP16 with higher-precision operations where needed to improve throughput and reduce memory usage. Mixed-precision training is noted as reducing memory consumption and increasing throughput. [2] Benefits include lower memory footprint, faster inference, and improved hardware efficiency. Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is a post-training tReinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is a post-training technique used to align AI models with human preferences. [1] RLHF is widely used to improve helpfulness, instruction following, and alignment with desired behaviors in modern AI systems. [1] In summary, tool use provides action capabilities, while planning coordinates those capabilities toward achieving goals autonomously. 314mixture of experts architectures and routing17:09:0823.54.617.02.011187yes
Mixture of Experts (MoE) is a model architecture that replaces some deMixture of Experts (MoE) is a model architecture that replaces some dense neural network layers with a collection of specialized subnetworks called experts. For each input token, only a small subset of experts is activated, allowing the model to scale to very large parameter counts while keeping computation per token relatively low. MoE training has been used successfully in models such as Mixtral and GLaM. [1] Routing is the mechanism that decides which expert(s) process each token. A routing network (or gate) scores the available experts and typically selects the top-k experts for each token. The selected experts process the token, and their outputs are combined. Effective routing aims to: - Send tokens to the most relevant experts. - Balance load across experts. - Avoid expert collapse, where only a few experts receive most traffic. In modern LLMs, sparse routing enables large model capacity without requiring every parameter to be used for every token, improving computational efficiency while maintaining strong performance. [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf"

318instruction tuning and preference optimization17:10:3517.33.911.32.111106yes
**Instruction Tuning** is a post-training method where a model is fine**Instruction Tuning** is a post-training method where a model is fine-tuned on instruction–response datasets so it learns to follow user requests, answer questions, and perform tasks consistently. Datasets may contain hundreds of thousands to millions of instruction examples and are commonly used to improve instruction-following behavior. [1] **Preference Optimization** trains a model using preference data that compares preferred versus rejected responses. One example is Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), which uses preference pairs created through prompt selection, response generation, and preference annotation. Models are often first trained with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and then optimized with DPO. [1] In the referenced Moxin-LLM workflow, post-training follows: - SFT (instruction tuning) - DPO (preference optimization) - Additional reasoning enhancement with reinforcement learning methods such as GRPO for Chain-of-Thought capabilities. 319inference time scaling and chain of thought reasoning17:10:5318.34.411.91.911334yes
**Inference-time scaling** refers to improving a model’s reasoning per**Inference-time scaling** refers to improving a model’s reasoning performance by allocating more computation during inference (test time), often by extending the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning process. The Moxin-LLM paper notes that OpenAI’s o1 series introduced inference-time scaling by increasing the length of CoT reasoning, leading to gains in mathematics, coding, and scientific reasoning tasks. [1] **Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning** is a technique where a model breaks a complex problem into intermediate reasoning steps. Research shows CoT can enhance reasoning, generalization, and learning efficiency by introducing sparse sequential dependencies and more interpretable attention patterns. In parity-learning experiments, transformers learned tasks with polynomial sample complexity using CoT, versus exponential sample complexity without it. [2] In short: CoT provides explicit intermediate reasoning steps, while inference-time scaling increases the amount of reasoning performed during inference, often through longer CoT processes. 320data quality and deduplication for model pretraining17:11:1322.73.815.92.911316yes
For model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical becFor model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical because duplicate or low-quality content can reduce training efficiency, increase memorization, and harm downstream performance. Key practices found in the knowledge base include: - Remove low-quality documents before training. One approach filters out very short or metadata-only documents, improving corpus quality. [1] - Apply exact and near-duplicate detection. Common methods include: - MinHash and Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH) for similarity detection. [1] - Jaccard similarity thresholds (e.g., 0.8–0.85) to identify near-duplicates. [1] - Deduplication both within and across data sources. [1] - Use quality filtering models after deduplication. Learnable filters such as fastText-based classifiers can retain only the highest-quality documents, improving downstream results. [1] - For code corpora, near-deduplication is especially important. In one dataset, about 40% of permissively licensed files were identified as near-duplicates and removed. [1] Research cited in the corpus also notes that deduplicating training data can improve language model quality and training efficiency. 322Which model extends Mistral from 32 to 36 transformer blocks? Describe its three pre-training phases, the context lengths in the first two phases, and the purpose of the final phase. Cite the supporting sections.17:12:0321.54.214.72.611428yes
The model is Moxin-7B. In its architecture section, the authors state The model is Moxin-7B. In its architecture section, the authors state that they extend the Mistral architecture and increase the depth from 32 blocks to 36 transformer blocks. Specifically: “While the original Mistral model comprises 32 blocks, our extended model uses 36 transformer blocks.” [1] Its pre-training is described as a three-phase process: • Phase 1 – Base Pre-training: - Trained with a fixed context length of 2,000 tokens. - Purpose: establish foundational language modeling abilities, including syntax and basic semantic patterns. [1] • Phase 2 – Extended Context Pre-training: - Context length increased to 4,000 tokens. - Purpose: enable the model to capture longer dependencies and richer context. [1] • Phase 3 – Capability Enhancement: - Incorporates capability-specific enhancement data collected to strengthen abilities such as reasoning, mathematical problem-solving, and knowledge memorization. - The paper notes that this phase uses capability-enhancement data and provides ablations comparing Phases 1–2 against all three phases to quantify the benefits. [1] Supporting sections: - “3.1 Model Architecture” for the 32→36 transformer-block extension. [1] - “3.4 Training Configuration” for the three pre-training phases and context lengths. 41retrieval augmented generation for large language models17:12:2616.14.311.80.111268yes
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is an approach that combines a laRetrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is an approach that combines a large language model (LLM) with an external retrieval system. Instead of relying only on information learned during training, the model first retrieves relevant documents or knowledge and then uses that information to generate a response. Benefits of RAG: - Improves factual accuracy by grounding responses in retrieved content. - Provides access to up-to-date or domain-specific information. - Reduces hallucinations compared to using an LLM alone. - Enables question answering over private or proprietary knowledge bases. A typical RAG workflow: - User submits a query. - A retrieval component searches a knowledge base for relevant documents. - Retrieved content is added to the model’s context. - The LLM generates an answer using both the query and retrieved information. The concept is related to retrieval-based question answering systems, where benchmarks such as ARC-C include questions that are difficult for retrieval-based and word co-occurrence algorithms alone, highlighting the value of combining retrieval with advanced language modeling. [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf"

43efficient fine tuning with low rank adaptation17:13:0116.84.911.01.01970yes
Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniLow-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique for large language models. Instead of updating all model weights, LoRA freezes the original model and trains small low-rank matrices that are injected into selected layers. This dramatically reduces trainable parameters, memory usage, and training cost. One example from the knowledge base describes parameter-efficient tuning using LoRA with rank r=64 and α=128, combined with INT4 quantization, targeting all linear modules during instruction tuning. [1] Benefits of LoRA include: - Lower GPU memory requirements. - Faster fine-tuning. - Smaller adapter files that can be shared separately from the base model. - Performance often close to full fine-tuning. LoRA is commonly used with quantization methods such as QLoRA for even greater efficiency. [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2408.11878v2.pdf "2408.11878v2.pdf"

46long context understanding and attention mechanisms17:14:0617.44.311.41.811567yes
Long-context understanding refers to a model’s ability to process and Long-context understanding refers to a model’s ability to process and reason over very long sequences while retaining relevant information from earlier parts of the input. Attention mechanisms are the core technique used by Transformers to determine which tokens in a sequence are most relevant when processing each token. Self-attention computes relationships between all token pairs, enabling the model to capture dependencies across a sequence. [1] For long contexts, standard attention becomes computationally expensive. Common optimizations include: - Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), which reduces memory usage by sharing key/value heads across groups of query heads. [2] - Sliding Window Attention (SWA), which restricts attention to a fixed-size window while still modeling long-range dependencies efficiently. A window size of 4096 can provide a theoretical attention span exceeding 14,000 tokens. [2] - Rolling buffer caches, which limit memory growth during inference by reusing storage for older tokens. [2] Research also shows that sparse and interpretable attention patterns can improve learning efficiency and reasoning performance. [3] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2402.16901v2.pdf "2402.16901v2.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2410.05459v2.pdf "2410.05459v2.pdf"
47knowledge distillation for smaller language models17:14:2422.54.716.51.411117yes
**Knowledge Distillation (KD)** is a technique for creating smaller, f**Knowledge Distillation (KD)** is a technique for creating smaller, faster models by transferring knowledge from a larger “teacher” model to a compact “student” model. The student learns not only from ground-truth labels but also from the teacher’s outputs or internal representations. [1] For smaller language models, KD helps retain much of the teacher model’s capability while reducing model size and inference cost. A common approach is teacher-student training using response-based distillation (teacher outputs) or feature-based distillation (intermediate representations). [1] The retrieved sources also note that smaller LLMs can be enhanced through techniques such as pruning, token reduction, and specialized training strategies, and that reasoning capabilities can be transferred using data distilled from stronger models. 414mixture of experts architectures and routing17:16:5626.84.020.42.411177yes
A Mixture of Experts (MoE) architecture is a neural network design thaA Mixture of Experts (MoE) architecture is a neural network design that contains multiple specialized sub-networks called “experts.” Instead of activating all parameters for every input, only a small subset of experts is used for each token or example, making the model computationally efficient while scaling to very large parameter counts. MoE training has been used successfully in models such as Mixtral and GLaM. [1] Routing is the mechanism that decides which expert(s) should process a given input. A routing network (or router) scores experts and selects one or more of them for each token. Common approaches include: - Top-1 routing: choose the highest-scoring expert. - Top-k routing: choose the k highest-scoring experts and combine their outputs. The goal of routing is to send different inputs to experts that have learned specialized capabilities, improving efficiency and model capacity while keeping computation manageable. The search results mention MoE as a training strategy and cite Mixtral and GLaM as examples. [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf"
415safety alignment and robustness of language models17:17:2418.53.713.31.611348yes
Safety alignment in language models refers to training and adapting moSafety alignment in language models refers to training and adapting models so their behavior follows desired principles, avoids harmful outputs, and remains helpful across diverse situations. One alignment approach described in the literature is Constitutional AI, where a model is trained to follow written principles and receives reinforcement-learning feedback from a “teacher AI” that identifies violations. This approach has been reported as effective against certain red-teaming attacks and harmful outputs. [1] Robustness refers to maintaining reliable performance under different conditions and resisting failures. Research highlights techniques such as careful data curation, quality filtering, deduplication, reinforcement learning, and rigorous evaluation to improve model behavior and reliability. Deduplication can reduce memorization and improve training efficiency, while post-training methods such as supervised fine-tuning (SFT), preference optimization (DPO), and reinforcement learning are used to develop more helpful and harmless assistants. 418instruction tuning and preference optimization17:18:2623.44.017.81.511248yes
Instruction tuning is a post-training technique where a pretrained lanInstruction tuning is a post-training technique where a pretrained language model is fine-tuned on datasets containing instructions and desired responses. The goal is to improve instruction-following behavior and performance on downstream tasks. For example, FinLLaMA-Instruct was developed through instruction fine-tuning using a 573K-sample instruction dataset to enhance instruction-following capabilities and domain-task performance. [1] Preference optimization is a post-training approach that uses preference data—pairs of preferred and rejected responses—to align model outputs with desired behaviors. One example is Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), where models are trained on preference datasets containing preferred/rejected response pairs generated and annotated through a preference pipeline. [2] In practice, instruction tuning teaches a model “how to follow instructions,” while preference optimization teaches it “which responses are preferred.” [2] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2408.11878v2.pdf "2408.11878v2.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf"
419inference time scaling and chain of thought reasoning17:18:5022.23.816.61.811302yes
Inference-time scaling refers to improving a model’s reasoning performInference-time scaling refers to improving a model’s reasoning performance by allocating more computation during inference (test time), often by extending or deepening the reasoning process rather than increasing model size. One source notes that OpenAI’s o1 series introduced inference-time scaling by increasing the length of the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning process, leading to significant gains in mathematics, coding, and scientific reasoning tasks. [1] Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning is a technique where a model breaks a complex problem into smaller, intermediate reasoning steps. Research describes CoT as improving reasoning, generalization, and learning efficiency by introducing step-by-step computations and sparse sequential dependencies that make problems easier for transformers to learn. [2] In short: CoT is the step-by-step reasoning process, while inference-time scaling is the broader strategy of spending more computation at inference time, often by generating longer or richer CoT reasoning. [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2410.05459v2.pdf "2410.05459v2.pdf"
420data quality and deduplication for model pretraining17:19:1423.54.016.43.111468yes
For model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical becFor model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical because duplicate or low-quality content can reduce training efficiency, increase memorization, and hurt generalization. Key practices identified in the source materials include: - Remove low-quality documents before training. For example, SlimPajama filters out very short documents that mainly contain metadata. [1] - Apply exact and near-duplicate detection using MinHash and Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH). SlimPajama performs deduplication both within and across data sources using MinHashLSH and Jaccard similarity thresholds. [1] - Use quality filtering models after deduplication. DCLM-BASELINE applies learned quality filters and retains only the highest-scoring documents. [1] - For code data, near-duplicate detection can remove large amounts of redundant content. The Stack dataset used MinHash, LSH, and Jaccard similarity, removing about 40% of files identified as duplicates or near-duplicates. [1] - Dataset creators report that deduplication improves training efficiency, reduces memorization, and lowers the risk of models reproducing training data verbatim. [1] A typical pipeline is: collect data → filter low-quality content → perform exact and near-duplicate removal → apply quality scoring/filtering → train on the curated corpus. [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf"

51retrieval augmented generation for large language models17:20:2224.34.319.90.211270yes
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is an approach that enhances LargRetrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is an approach that enhances Large Language Models (LLMs) by retrieving relevant external information and supplying it as context before generating a response. How RAG works: - A user submits a query. - A retrieval system searches documents, databases, or knowledge sources for relevant information. - The retrieved content is provided to the LLM as additional context. - The LLM generates an answer grounded in the retrieved information. Benefits: - Reduces hallucinations by grounding responses in retrieved data. - Provides access to up-to-date or domain-specific knowledge without retraining the model. - Improves transparency because answers can be linked to source documents. - Enables organizations to use private knowledge bases with LLMs. RAG is commonly used in enterprise search, customer support, document question-answering, and AI assistants that need access to organizational knowledge. Evaluation benchmarks for LLMs often include retrieval-based question-answering tasks, such as ARC-C, which contains questions difficult for retrieval and word co-occurrence algorithms. [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf"
52reducing hallucinations in language model responses17:20:4827.03.919.13.911608yes
Reducing hallucinations in language model responses can be approached Reducing hallucinations in language model responses can be approached through data quality, reasoning methods, and evaluation. Key practices include: - Use high-quality, filtered, and deduplicated training data. Deduplication and quality filtering can improve training efficiency and reduce memorization-related issues. [1] - Fine-tune models with instruction-following datasets and preference optimization methods (such as SFT and DPO) to improve response quality and alignment. [1] - Incorporate Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. Research shows CoT can improve sample efficiency, create more interpretable attention patterns, and enhance reasoning performance. [2] - Use reinforcement learning with outcome-based rewards that verify correctness of answers rather than rewarding intermediate steps. [1] - Evaluate models with benchmarks covering reasoning, knowledge, and truthfulness to identify weaknesses and track improvements. [1] - Monitor hallucination behavior directly; hallucination in natural language generation is recognized as an important research area requiring dedicated analysis and mitigation. [3] These techniques are often combined in modern LLM development pipelines. 53efficient fine tuning with low rank adaptation17:21:1618.54.112.61.811179yes
Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniLow-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that adapts a pre-trained model by training small low-rank matrices instead of updating all model parameters. This significantly reduces memory usage and training cost while preserving most of the model’s original knowledge. A cited example of efficient fine-tuning uses QLoRA with LoRA settings of: - Rank (r) = 64 - Alpha (α) = 128 - No dropout - INT4 quantization - Training only adapter parameters while keeping the base model largely unchanged [1] In the Open-FinLLMs project, instruction tuning was performed using QLoRA, achieving parameter-efficient tuning with these LoRA settings and quantization techniques during fine-tuning of an 8B-parameter model. [1] The main benefits of LoRA are: - Lower GPU memory requirements - Faster fine-tuning - Smaller task-specific checkpoints - Comparable performance to full fine-tuning in many tasks QLoRA extends LoRA by combining it with model quantization, further reducing memory consumption during training. 56long context understanding and attention mechanisms17:22:2121.14.415.01.711343yes
Long-context understanding refers to a model’s ability to process, retLong-context understanding refers to a model’s ability to process, retain, and reason over information spanning thousands of tokens. One approach described in the knowledge base combines: - Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), which shares key/value heads across groups of query heads to reduce memory usage while maintaining representational power. [1] - Sliding Window Attention (SWA), which limits attention computations to a moving window, enabling efficient processing of long sequences while preserving long-range dependencies. A window size of 4096 can provide a theoretical attention span exceeding 14,000 tokens. [1] - Rolling Buffer Cache, which reuses storage for keys and values during inference, reducing memory usage on 32K-token sequences by about 8×. [1] Attention mechanisms work by transforming inputs into Query (Q), Key (K), and Value (V) vectors, computing attention scores between tokens, applying a softmax normalization, and producing weighted outputs. Multi-head attention extends this by learning multiple attention patterns simultaneously. 57knowledge distillation for smaller language models17:22:4323.84.218.11.511166yes
Knowledge distillation (KD) is a technique that transfers knowledge frKnowledge distillation (KD) is a technique that transfers knowledge from a larger “teacher” model to a smaller “student” model, enabling the student to be more compact and efficient while retaining much of the teacher’s performance. KD commonly follows a teacher–student architecture and can transfer knowledge through output responses (response-based distillation) or internal layer representations (feature-based distillation). [1] For smaller language models, distillation is often used to improve reasoning and task performance. One example describes a 7B model being fine-tuned with chain-of-thought data distilled from a stronger reasoning model, improving reasoning capabilities while keeping the model relatively small. [2] The main goal is to create lightweight models that are faster and cheaper to deploy while preserving as much capability as possible from larger models. 58quantization techniques for efficient model inference17:23:0822.13.717.90.511248yes
Quantization reduces model precision (for example, from FP32 to INT8 oQuantization reduces model precision (for example, from FP32 to INT8 or INT4) to improve inference speed, reduce memory usage, and lower deployment costs. Common techniques include: - Post-Training Quantization (PTQ): Converts a trained model to lower precision without retraining. - Quantization-Aware Training (QAT): Simulates quantization during training to preserve accuracy after deployment. - Dynamic Quantization: Quantizes weights ahead of time and activations at runtime. - Static Quantization: Quantizes both weights and activations using calibration data. - Mixed-Precision Quantization: Uses different precisions for different layers to balance speed and accuracy. Examples from the knowledge base include an INT8-quantized ASR model ("Reverb Turbo") designed for applications sensitive to speed and memory usage. [1] Quantization is also referenced as an optimization path for accelerating inference alongside techniques such as knowledge distillation. Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is a post-training tReinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is a post-training technique used to align AI models with human preferences. It combines reinforcement learning with feedback from people who evaluate or rank model outputs. [1] RLHF aims to make models more helpful, safer, and better aligned with user expectations by learning from human judgments rather than relying only on next-token prediction. 514mixture of experts architectures and routing17:25:1523.64.217.91.511184yes
Mixture of Experts (MoE) is a neural network architecture that replaceMixture of Experts (MoE) is a neural network architecture that replaces a single large feed-forward network with many specialized “expert” subnetworks. For each input token, only a small subset of experts is activated, which allows the model to scale to very large parameter counts while keeping computation relatively low. Models such as Mixtral and GLaM are cited as successful examples of MoE training for improving smaller-model performance and efficient scaling. [1] Routing is the mechanism that decides which expert(s) process a given token. A router (or gating network) scores the experts and sends each token to the top-ranked experts. Effective routing balances: - Specialization: experts learn different skills. - Load balancing: traffic is spread across experts. - Efficiency: only a few experts are active per token. In practice, modern MoE models commonly use top-k routing, where each token is sent to the highest-scoring experts rather than all experts. This enables sparse computation while retaining large model capacity. [1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf"
515safety alignment and robustness of language models17:25:3917.13.811.32.111437yes
Safety alignment and robustness are two central goals in language modeSafety alignment and robustness are two central goals in language model development. - Safety alignment focuses on ensuring models behave according to intended values, policies, and human preferences. Techniques include reinforcement learning, instruction tuning, preference optimization, and Constitutional AI, where models are trained to follow specified principles and avoid harmful outputs. Constitutional AI has been reported as effective against some red-teaming attacks and harmful content generation. [1] - Robustness refers to a model’s ability to remain reliable under challenging conditions, such as adversarial prompts, distribution shifts, noisy inputs, or long-context tasks. Robustness is supported through data curation, deduplication, quality filtering, careful training procedures, and evaluation across diverse benchmarks. Deduplication can reduce memorization and improve training efficiency. [2] Recent work also explores alignment through public participation, collecting values and preferences from diverse groups to help guide AI behavior, though researchers note challenges in translating broad principles into concrete actions. **Instruction Tuning** is a supervised fine-tuning process where a pre**Instruction Tuning** is a supervised fine-tuning process where a pretrained model is trained on instruction-response examples to improve instruction-following behavior and downstream task performance. 519inference time scaling and chain of thought reasoning17:27:2416.54.110.51.911209yes
Inference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s reasoning peInference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s reasoning performance by allocating more computation during inference (test time), often by allowing longer or more elaborate reasoning processes. One source states that OpenAI’s o1 series introduced inference-time scaling by increasing the length of the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning process, leading to improvements in mathematics, coding, and scientific reasoning tasks. [1] Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning is a technique where a model breaks a complex problem into smaller intermediate steps. Research describes CoT as improving reasoning, generalization, and sample efficiency by introducing step-by-step reasoning and sparse sequential dependencies between tokens. [2] In short: - Chain-of-Thought = the step-by-step reasoning process. [2] - Inference-time scaling = spending more computation at inference, often by generating longer or richer CoT reasoning. 520data quality and deduplication for model pretraining17:27:4221.14.313.63.111447yes
For model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical becFor model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical because duplicate or low-quality data can reduce training efficiency, increase memorization, and hurt generalization. Key practices identified in the sources include: - Remove low-quality content before training. SlimPajama filters out short or low-information documents and then performs extensive deduplication to improve data quality and efficiency. [1] - Use exact and near-duplicate detection. Techniques such as MinHash, MinHashLSH, Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH), suffix-array pipelines, Bloom filtering, and Jaccard similarity thresholds are used to identify duplicate and near-duplicate documents. [1] - Deduplicate across data sources, not just within each source. Inter-source duplicates can be substantial and negatively affect training. [1] - Apply quality filtering after deduplication. DCLM-BASELINE retains only top-quality documents using learned quality filters. [1] - For code datasets, near-deduplication is especially important. One example removed about 40% of permissively licensed code files after detecting near-duplicates with Jaccard similarity ≥ 0.85. [1] Research cited in the sources notes that deduplication improves training efficiency, reduces memorization, and can improve downstream model quality. 11retrieval augmented generation for large language models16:45:4111.24.05.91.311221yes
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances LargRetrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances Large Language Models (LLMs) by retrieving relevant external information and providing it as context before generating a response. It is designed for knowledge-intensive tasks and helps reduce hallucinations while improving factual accuracy. [1] A typical RAG workflow is: - Retrieve relevant documents from a knowledge base. - Re-rank or filter the retrieved results. - Provide the retrieved content to the LLM as context. - Generate an answer grounded in that information. [2] RAG is used in domains such as healthcare, scientific research, and knowledge retrieval, where access to up-to-date or specialized information is important. It can improve smaller LLMs by leveraging external knowledge sources and preprocessing techniques. [3] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_03/2410.19599v3.pdf "2410.19599v3.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2409.15076v1.pdf "2409.15076v1.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_08/2412.02868v2.pdf "2412.02868v2.pdf"
12reducing hallucinations in language model responses16:45:5414.66.25.82.612036yes
Reducing hallucinations in language model responses typically involvesReducing hallucinations in language model responses typically involves grounding outputs in reliable information and improving reasoning processes. Key approaches include: - Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG): Incorporate external knowledge sources during generation, which helps reduce errors caused by outdated information or unsupported claims. [1]​[2] - Structured reasoning: Step-by-step instructions and Chain-of-Thought prompting can improve accuracy and reduce hallucinations in some tasks. [1]​[3] - Alignment and grounding techniques: Methods that align generation with available context before producing answers can mitigate hallucinations. [4] - Debiasing and calibration: Reducing model biases can improve grounding and lower hallucination rates. [5] - Abstention mechanisms: Allowing models to acknowledge knowledge gaps rather than guessing can help prevent fabricated answers. [6] Research also notes that hallucinations may be difficult to eliminate completely in current LLM architectures, making detection and mitigation especially important. 13efficient fine tuning with low rank adaptation16:46:1110.63.75.81.01993yes
Efficient fine-tuning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-eEfficient fine-tuning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient method for adapting large pre-trained models. Instead of updating all model weights, LoRA freezes the original weights and inserts small trainable low-rank matrices into selected layers. During training, only these low-rank matrices are updated. [1] Benefits include: - Much fewer trainable parameters than full fine-tuning. [2] - Lower computational and memory requirements. [2] - Reduced risk of catastrophic forgetting of the model’s original knowledge. [2] Mathematically, a frozen weight matrix W is adapted as W + ABᵀ, where A and B are low-rank trainable matrices and the rank r is much smaller than the original dimensions. [2] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_02/2403.05887v2.pdf "2403.05887v2.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2412.20772v2.pdf "2412.20772v2.pdf"

15multimodal learning combining vision and language16:46:3612.05.55.21.214079yes
Multimodal learning that combines vision and language aims to train moMultimodal learning that combines vision and language aims to train models to understand and reason across both images and text. A common approach is to align visual and textual representations in a shared embedding space, enabling tasks such as image captioning, visual question answering, image retrieval, and text-to-image generation. CLIP is a notable example that learns relationships between images and text through contrastive learning. [2] Models such as Flamingo incorporate visual tokens with textual encodings through cross-attention layers, enabling joint vision-language understanding. [3] Recent multimodal large language models extend these capabilities to both visual understanding and generation tasks. 16long context understanding and attention mechanisms16:46:4911.24.25.31.711579yes
Long-context understanding is an AI model’s ability to process, retainLong-context understanding is an AI model’s ability to process, retain, and reason over large amounts of information across very long inputs. Longer contexts can help with tasks such as document retrieval, research, legal analysis, and understanding information spread across many sources. However, performance can vary as context length grows, and retrieval accuracy may decline in some parts of very long inputs. [1] Attention mechanisms are the core components of transformer models. Self-attention computes the relevance of each token to other tokens, allowing the model to weigh and integrate information from different positions in a sequence and capture contextual dependencies. Multi-head attention enables the model to attend to multiple aspects of the input simultaneously. [2]​[3] To support long contexts efficiently, models may use techniques such as Grouped-Query Attention (GQA) and Sliding Window Attention (SWA), which reduce memory and computational costs while maintaining long-range context handling. 17knowledge distillation for smaller language models16:47:0211.03.75.02.314404yes
Knowledge distillation is a model compression technique where a large Knowledge distillation is a model compression technique where a large “teacher” model transfers knowledge to a smaller “student” model. The student is trained to mimic the teacher’s behavior, enabling significant compression and acceleration while retaining much of the teacher’s performance. [1]​[2] For language models, distillation has been used to compress large models such as BERT into smaller variants like TinyBERT through a teacher-student training process. [3]​[4] Research has also explored: - Layer-wise distillation for language model compression. [3] - Knowledge distillation for pre-training language models (MiniPLM). [5] - Distilling reasoning processes so smaller models can outperform larger models with less training data. [6] The main goal is to create smaller, faster, and more efficient language models while preserving capabilities learned by larger models.
