18quantization techniques for efficient model inference16:47:1412.34.75.32.412083yes
**Quantization Techniques for Efficient Model Inference** Quantizatio**Quantization Techniques for Efficient Model Inference** Quantization reduces model precision (for example, from FP32 to INT8 or INT4) to decrease memory usage and improve inference speed while maintaining acceptable accuracy. Common techniques include: - **Post-Training Quantization (PTQ):** Quantizes a trained model without retraining. An example is 8-bit asymmetric per-channel quantization, which can achieve little to no accuracy loss in some applications. [1] - **Uniform Quantization:** Maps values into evenly spaced levels using a fixed step size. [2]​[3] - **Non-Uniform Quantization:** Uses data-aware mappings such as NF4 (NormalFloat 4-bit), which assumes normally distributed weights and can improve low-bit quantization quality. [2] - **Weight-Only Quantization:** Examples include W4A16 and W8A8 configurations used to accelerate inference while preserving accuracy. [4] - **Quantization-Aware Fine-Tuning:** Combines quantization with adapter-based tuning (such as LoRA) to reduce the gap between quantized and full-precision models. [2] Studies report that INT8 quantization can significantly reduce inference latency with only minor accuracy impact, and 8-bit inference often performs similarly to full-precision models. [5]​[6] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_03/2408.12481v2.pdf "2408.12481v2.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2412.20772v2.pdf "2412.20772v2.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_08/2408.10501v2.pdf "2408.10501v2.pdf" [4]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_07/2412.04468v2.pdf "2412.04468v2.pdf" [5]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2407.11061v2.pdf "2407.11061v2.pdf" [6]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_10/2406.17972v3.pdf "2406.17972v3.pdf"
19reinforcement learning from human feedback16:47:2811.04.85.60.61892yes
Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is a technique used Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is a technique used to align AI models with human preferences. It typically involves two stages: • Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), where the model learns desired behaviors from example demonstrations. • A reinforcement learning stage that uses human preference data—comparisons between preferred and rejected responses—to help the model distinguish better outputs from worse ones. [1] RLHF has become a primary approach for making language models more helpful and less harmful, and it has contributed significantly to the success of systems such as ChatGPT. 112tool use and planning in autonomous AI agents16:48:0412.75.65.31.811467yes
Autonomous AI agents typically rely on two core capabilities: - Tool Autonomous AI agents typically rely on two core capabilities: - Tool use: Agents interact with external tools such as search engines, databases, APIs, code executors, or specialized software to gather information and perform actions. ReAct-style prompting allows agents to refine outputs based on previous results and use external resources dynamically. [1] - Planning: Agents decompose complex goals into smaller tasks, determine execution order, and adapt based on outcomes. Research has highlighted context-aware planning mechanisms that outperform rigid predefined workflows. [2] Modern agent systems often combine planning with tool invocation, where a central agent processes requests and selects appropriate tools or sub-workflows to achieve objectives. [3] Multi-agent frameworks further extend this by coordinating specialized agents to solve distinct sub-problems and improve overall performance. 113improving factual accuracy through retrieval16:48:1811.84.75.41.711451yes
Retrieval can improve factual accuracy by grounding generated answers Retrieval can improve factual accuracy by grounding generated answers in relevant source material rather than relying only on a model’s internal knowledge. Key techniques include: - Retrieving the most relevant documents or passages before generation and using them as context. This can improve response quality and accuracy. [1] - Using similarity-based retrieval to select examples or knowledge that closely match the user’s query, improving both accuracy and efficiency. [2] - Adding a re-ranking stage after initial retrieval. Cross-encoder re-rankers evaluate the query and retrieved documents together, improving retrieval precision and output quality. [1] - Evaluating faithfulness by checking whether generated claims are supported by retrieved sources. [1] Challenges remain: irrelevant retrieved content can introduce noise and reduce accuracy, so retrieval quality is critical. 114mixture of experts architectures and routing16:48:3112.13.65.63.011364yes
Mixture of Experts (MoE) is a neural network architecture that combineMixture of Experts (MoE) is a neural network architecture that combines multiple specialized sub-models called experts. Instead of activating all experts for every input, an MoE uses a routing or gating mechanism to select only the most relevant experts, enabling much larger model capacity with relatively low computation. [1] Routing is the process that determines which experts handle a given input. The router computes similarity or relevance scores between the input and available experts, then assigns weights or selects a subset of experts (often top-k experts) for processing. [1] Key points: - MoE uses specialized experts rather than a single dense model. [1] - Sparse MoE routes each input to only a subset of experts, allowing many parameters with nearly constant computational cost. [1] - Routing is typically implemented through a trainable gating network or routing layer. [2] - Common routing approaches include linear routers and cosine routers, which score expert relevance differently. [1] - Modern LLMs such as Mixtral and DBRX are examples of MoE-based models. [2] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2405.14131v3.pdf "2405.14131v3.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_05/2406.02969v2.pdf "2406.02969v2.pdf"
115safety alignment and robustness of language models16:48:4510.74.04.32.411467yes
**Safety Alignment and Robustness of Language Models** Safety alignme**Safety Alignment and Robustness of Language Models** Safety alignment aims to make language models behave consistently with human values and avoid harmful outputs. Common alignment methods include supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF). [1] Robustness refers to a model's ability to maintain safe and reliable behavior under challenging conditions, including adversarial prompts, jailbreak attempts, and distribution shifts. Research shows that even safety-aligned models can remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks and adversarial manipulations. [2] Recent work highlights challenges such as: - Safety alignment failures caused by limitations in preference-optimization methods. [1] - Vulnerability to universal and transferable adversarial attacks and jailbreaks. [3] - The need for more comprehensive safety-alignment frameworks and stronger evaluation methods. 118instruction tuning and preference optimization16:49:2310.93.74.72.511753yes
**Instruction Tuning** is a post-training method where a model is trai**Instruction Tuning** is a post-training method where a model is trained on instruction-response pairs to improve its ability to follow human instructions and perform downstream tasks. It is typically optimized using maximum likelihood estimation on curated instruction datasets. [1]​[2] **Preference Optimization** is a model alignment approach that learns from preference data, where one response is preferred over another. Methods such as Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and SimPO adjust the model to favor preferred outputs without requiring traditional reinforcement learning. [3]​[4] A common training pipeline is: - First, instruction tuning to improve instruction-following behavior. - Then, preference optimization (e.g., DPO) to align outputs with human preferences. [5] Research cited in the knowledge base notes that preference optimization methods include DPO, SimPO, IPO, and related variants. [3]​[6] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_07/2407.18271v4.pdf "2407.18271v4.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2408.11878v2.pdf "2408.11878v2.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_04/2410.13828v2.pdf "2410.13828v2.pdf" [4]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_05/2411.00816v2.pdf "2411.00816v2.pdf" [5]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf" [6]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_08/2405.14953v5.pdf "2405.14953v5.pdf"
119inference time scaling and chain of thought reasoning16:49:3511.84.15.62.111616yes
**Inference-Time Scaling** refers to improving a model’s performance b**Inference-Time Scaling** refers to improving a model’s performance by using more computation during inference (test time), rather than only making the model larger or training it longer. One approach is to increase the length or depth of the reasoning process during inference. Research cited in the knowledge base notes that OpenAI’s o1 series introduced inference-time scaling by increasing the length of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, leading to improvements in math, coding, and scientific reasoning tasks. [1] **Chain-of-Thought (CoT) Reasoning** is a prompting and reasoning technique that encourages a model to break a problem into intermediate steps and reason sequentially before producing a final answer. It has been shown to improve reasoning performance and is widely used for tasks requiring multi-step logic, mathematics, coding, and decision-making. [2]​[3]​[4] The connection is that inference-time scaling often uses longer or richer CoT reasoning traces to spend more compute on difficult problems, which can improve task performance. 120data quality and deduplication for model pretraining16:49:4811.23.85.51.91994yes
For model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical becFor model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical because duplicated or low-quality data can increase memorization, reduce diversity, and waste training compute. Research cited in the knowledge base notes that deduplication improves training efficiency and reduces the likelihood of models simply recalling training examples. [1] Common practices include: - Removing short, low-information, or noisy documents before training. [1] - Performing exact and near-duplicate detection using MinHash, Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH), and Jaccard similarity thresholds. [1] - Deduplicating both within and across data sources. [1] - Applying quality filters and retaining only the highest-quality documents. [1] Large datasets such as SlimPajama and The Stack use these techniques extensively to improve data quality and training outcomes. 21retrieval augmented generation for large language models16:50:2610.83.95.21.711212yes
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that improves LargRetrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that improves Large Language Models (LLMs) by retrieving relevant information from external knowledge sources and adding it to the prompt before generating a response. This helps reduce hallucinations, incorporate up-to-date information, and provide answers grounded in verifiable sources. [1] RAG combines: - Parametric knowledge: information stored in the LLM's trained parameters. - Non-parametric knowledge: external documents, databases, or repositories retrieved at query time. [2] Benefits include: - Easier knowledge updates without retraining the model. - Improved transparency because retrieved sources can be inspected. - Reduced hallucinations and false extrapolation. [2] A typical RAG workflow retrieves relevant documents, inserts them into the model context, and then generates an answer using both the retrieved content and the model’s reasoning capabilities. 22reducing hallucinations in language model responses16:50:3810.94.24.72.011403yes
Research in the knowledge base highlights several approaches that can Research in the knowledge base highlights several approaches that can reduce hallucinations: - Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) injects external knowledge into prompts, helping address errors caused by outdated information or unsupported model knowledge. [1]​[2] - Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting can improve response accuracy and reduce hallucinations in some tasks, though its effectiveness varies by model and domain. [3] - Grounding responses in retrieved knowledge bases can produce more coherent answers and mitigate hallucinations from internal model knowledge alone. [2] - Abstention and knowledge-gap detection have been explored as ways to avoid answering when the model lacks sufficient knowledge. [4] - Hallucination detection and mitigation techniques are active research areas with dedicated benchmarks and evaluation methods. 23efficient fine tuning with low rank adaptation16:50:5012.04.85.41.811580yes
Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning methodLow-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that freezes a pretrained model’s original weights and injects small trainable low-rank matrices into model layers. Instead of updating all parameters, only these low-rank adapters are trained, significantly reducing memory and computational requirements. [1] Benefits include: - Lower training cost and memory usage because the base model remains frozen. [1]​[2] - Efficient adaptation of large language models to new tasks using a small number of trainable parameters. [1]​[3] - Compatibility with quantization approaches, which can further reduce storage needs during fine-tuning. [1] LoRA has been applied across LLMs, speech, vision-language, and other domains. **Multimodal learning combining vision and language** is an AI approac**Multimodal learning combining vision and language** is an AI approach that learns from both visual data (images, videos) and textual data simultaneously. It aims to align and integrate information from these modalities into a shared representation, enabling systems to understand relationships between what is seen and what is described in language. 26long context understanding and attention mechanisms16:51:2811.74.34.82.611910yes
**Long Context Understanding and Attention Mechanisms** Long-context **Long Context Understanding and Attention Mechanisms** Long-context understanding refers to a model’s ability to process, retain, retrieve, and reason over information distributed across very large input sequences. Longer contexts can improve tasks such as legal document analysis, academic research, and retrieval across many documents. [1] Attention mechanisms are the core component that enable this capability. In Transformers, each token generates query, key, and value vectors. Self-attention computes relevance scores between tokens, allowing the model to focus on the most relevant information and build contextual understanding. [2]​[3] Challenges include: - Attention’s quadratic time and memory complexity with sequence length. [4] - Reduced retrieval accuracy in the middle of very long contexts. [1] - Difficulty extrapolating to sequences longer than those seen during training. [4] Common solutions include Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), Sliding Window Attention (SWA), and memory/retrieval workflows that improve efficiency for long-context processing. [5]​[6] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_10/2411.05000v2.pdf "2411.05000v2.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_05/2404.05101v3.pdf "2404.05101v3.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_04/2411.08932v2.pdf "2411.08932v2.pdf" [4]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2411.08909v3.pdf "2411.08909v3.pdf" [5]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf" [6]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_08/2410.08143v2.pdf "2410.08143v2.pdf"
27knowledge distillation for smaller language models16:51:4011.34.25.11.914255yes
Knowledge distillation is a model compression technique where a large Knowledge distillation is a model compression technique where a large “teacher” model transfers knowledge to a smaller “student” model. The student is trained to mimic the teacher’s behavior, allowing it to achieve strong performance with fewer parameters and lower computational cost. [1] For language models, distillation is commonly used to compress large models into smaller versions. Examples include: - TinyBERT, which distills BERT into a much smaller model for natural language understanding. [2]​[3] - MiniPLM, a knowledge-distillation approach for pre-training language models. [4] Research also shows that step-by-step distillation can help smaller language models outperform larger ones while using less training data. [5] Benefits include reduced memory usage, faster inference, and lower deployment costs while retaining much of the teacher model’s capability. 28quantization techniques for efficient model inference16:51:5311.13.85.02.411345yes
Quantization reduces model precision (for example, from 32-bit to 8-biQuantization reduces model precision (for example, from 32-bit to 8-bit or 4-bit) to lower memory use and speed up inference while often maintaining similar performance. [1] Common techniques mentioned in the knowledge base include: - 8-bit quantization: Model weights are quantized to 8-bit for faster inference and lower GPU memory usage, with little performance degradation reported. [1] - W8A8 quantization: Uses 8-bit weights and 8-bit activations to reduce compute latency, particularly in vision encoders. [2] - W4A16 quantization: Uses 4-bit weights and 16-bit activations to accelerate memory-bound decoding workloads. [2] - AWQ and GPTQ: Quantization methods widely used for large language models; AWQ has also been applied to vision-language models. [2] - Post-Training Quantization (PTQ): Converts trained models to lower precision (e.g., 8-bit) without retraining and can use asymmetric per-channel quantization. 29reinforcement learning from human feedback16:52:0610.04.15.00.91907yes
**Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF)** is a method used**Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF)** is a method used to align AI models with human preferences. It typically involves two stages: - Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), where demonstration data teaches desired behaviors. - A reinforcement learning stage that uses human preference data—comparisons between responses—to help the model distinguish preferred from rejected outputs and learn better behavior. [1] RLHF has become a primary approach for making language models more helpful and less harmful. [1] It is also widely used in post-training large language models to refine behavior and improve capabilities. 212tool use and planning in autonomous AI agents16:52:4310.74.94.11.711669yes
Tool use and planning are core capabilities of autonomous AI agents. Tool use and planning are core capabilities of autonomous AI agents. • Tool use enables agents to interact with external resources such as APIs, databases, search engines, code executors, and specialized software. Frameworks cited in the literature describe agents selecting tools, executing actions, observing results, and iterating until a task is completed. [1] • Planning allows agents to decompose complex goals into smaller tasks, choose appropriate actions, and adapt based on feedback. Research has shown benefits from context-aware planning rather than rigid predefined workflows. [2] • Modern agent architectures often combine reasoning, planning, memory, and tool use. LLM agents are described as having capabilities for reasoning, planning, and tool utilization, sometimes enhanced by memory systems and iterative feedback mechanisms. [3]​[4] • Emerging research explores agents that can create and refine their own tools when existing ones are insufficient. 213improving factual accuracy through retrieval16:52:5511.64.55.51.611630yes
Improving factual accuracy through retrieval is commonly achieved withImproving factual accuracy through retrieval is commonly achieved with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), where relevant information is retrieved from trusted sources and supplied to the model before generating an answer. Key findings from the retrieved sources: - Retrieval-based methods improve accuracy by selecting the most relevant examples or knowledge instead of relying only on the model’s internal knowledge. Similarity-based retrieval is often used to find the best supporting information. [1] - The retrieval step is considered critical for high-quality responses, and separating information into focused domains can improve retrieval accuracy. [2] - Adding a re-ranking stage after initial retrieval can further improve quality by evaluating the query and retrieved documents together, enabling better contextual matching. [2] - Faithfulness can be evaluated by checking whether generated claims are supported by the retrieved sources. [2] - Retrieval quality matters: too much retrieved content may introduce irrelevant information and reduce accuracy. 214mixture of experts architectures and routing16:53:0811.44.54.92.011299yes
Mixture of Experts (MoE) is an architecture that combines multiple speMixture of Experts (MoE) is an architecture that combines multiple specialized models (“experts”) with a routing mechanism that decides which experts should process a given input. Instead of using fixed mixture weights, the router computes input-dependent weights and assigns more importance to relevant experts. [1] Modern sparse MoE architectures route each input token to only a subset of experts, allowing model capacity to scale while keeping computation relatively constant. This approach is widely used in large language models and other domains. [1] Routing mechanisms include: - Linear routing: computes similarity scores between token representations and expert embeddings. [1] - Cosine routing: an alternative designed to reduce representation collapse, where only a few experts dominate decisions. [1] - Top-k gating: commonly selects the highest-ranked experts using a trainable gating network. [2] Routing is the key component that determines expert selection and load distribution across experts. [1]​[2] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2405.14131v3.pdf "2405.14131v3.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_09/2408.04307v3.pdf "2408.04307v3.pdf"
215safety alignment and robustness of language models16:53:2110.23.95.21.111210yes
Safety alignment aims to make language models behave consistently withSafety alignment aims to make language models behave consistently with human values, ethical standards, and intended policies. Common alignment methods include supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), and safety-focused training. Research also explores techniques such as Safe RLHF, inference-time alignment, and backdoor-enhanced safety alignment to improve harmlessness and resistance to misuse. [1] Robustness refers to a model's ability to remain reliable under adversarial inputs, jailbreak attempts, prompt manipulation, data contamination, or other attacks. Studies have shown that even safety-aligned models can remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks and adversarial prompts, highlighting the need for stronger defenses and more comprehensive safety frameworks. [2] Current research focuses on improving both alignment and robustness while preserving model utility and performance. 217embedding models for semantic document retrieval16:53:4511.34.15.91.311091yes
For semantic document retrieval, the knowledge base highlights dense rFor semantic document retrieval, the knowledge base highlights dense retrieval approaches that use embeddings to capture semantic meaning rather than keyword matches. 218instruction tuning and preference optimization16:53:5710.13.74.71.711666yes
Instruction tuning and preference optimization are two key post-trainiInstruction tuning and preference optimization are two key post-training stages for large language models. • Instruction tuning trains a model on instruction-response examples so it can better follow user requests and perform downstream tasks. It is typically done with supervised fine-tuning on curated datasets. [1]​[2] • Preference optimization aligns a model with preferred behaviors using preference data (for example, chosen vs. rejected responses). Common methods include Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and SimPO. [3]​[4] A common training pipeline is: 1. Supervised Fine-Tuning (instruction tuning). 2. Preference optimization (such as DPO) on preference datasets. [5] The goal of instruction tuning is task following, while preference optimization focuses on aligning outputs with human or desired preferences. 219inference time scaling and chain of thought reasoning16:54:0810.53.55.61.411520yes
**Inference-time scaling** refers to improving a model’s performance b**Inference-time scaling** refers to improving a model’s performance by allocating more computation during inference (test time) rather than increasing model size or training data. One approach is to increase reasoning effort or explore more candidate solutions during generation. Research cited in the knowledge base notes that increasing inference-time computation can improve performance by providing better coverage of the search space. [1] **Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning** is a prompting or reasoning technique where a model breaks a problem into intermediate steps before producing an answer. CoT has been shown to elicit reasoning in large language models and can improve performance on tasks involving math, logic, coding, and other multi-step problems. [2]​[3] The knowledge base also notes that some recent reasoning models use inference-time scaling by increasing the length of the CoT reasoning process, leading to gains on reasoning benchmarks. 220data quality and deduplication for model pretraining16:54:2011.44.15.81.511088yes
For model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical becFor model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical because duplicated or low-quality data can reduce training efficiency, increase memorization, and hurt generalization. Key practices include: - Removing low-quality documents, such as very short records or content containing mostly metadata. [1] - Applying exact and near-duplicate detection across all data sources, not just within individual sources. [1] - Using MinHash and Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH) with Jaccard similarity thresholds to identify near-duplicates. [1] - Filtering data with quality classifiers and language identification models to retain the highest-quality content. [1]​[2] Reported benefits include improved training efficiency, reduced memorization, lower redundancy, and better downstream model performance. 31retrieval augmented generation for large language models16:54:5810.43.94.91.711381yes
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances LargRetrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances Large Language Models (LLMs) by retrieving relevant external information and providing it as context before generating a response. This helps improve factual accuracy, reduces hallucinations, and allows models to use knowledge beyond their training data. [1] A foundational RAG approach was introduced by Patrick Lewis and colleagues in “Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP Tasks” (NeurIPS 2020). [2] Typical RAG workflow: - Retrieve relevant documents from a knowledge base. - Rank or rerank the retrieved content. - Supply the retrieved context to the LLM. - Generate an answer grounded in that information. [3] RAG is widely used in domains such as healthcare, scientific literature analysis, and knowledge retrieval systems. [4]​[3] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2407.02994v3.pdf "2407.02994v3.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_05/2411.00816v2.pdf "2411.00816v2.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2409.15076v1.pdf "2409.15076v1.pdf" [4]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_08/2412.02868v2.pdf "2412.02868v2.pdf"
32reducing hallucinations in language model responses16:55:1011.03.84.72.612021yes
Reducing hallucinations in language model responses typically focuses Reducing hallucinations in language model responses typically focuses on improving grounding, retrieval, reasoning, and evaluation. Key approaches supported by the knowledge base include: - Ground responses in verified sources using Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). Retrieved knowledge can provide relevant context and mitigate hallucinations caused by relying only on a model’s internal knowledge. [1] - Ground predictions in observed data or evidence. Research notes that grounding outputs in observed information reduces the likelihood of hallucinations. [2] - Use Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting carefully. Studies found CoT can improve accuracy and reduce hallucinations in some tasks, though it may occasionally reinforce incorrect reasoning. [3] - Address model biases and apply debiasing techniques, which have been identified as a promising direction for reducing hallucinations. [4] - Continuously evaluate and refine models using feedback, testing, and reliability-focused improvements. [5] The knowledge base also notes that hallucinations remain a fundamental challenge for current large language models and may arise during data preparation, training, or inference. 33efficient fine tuning with low rank adaptation16:55:2210.53.85.51.211187yes
Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniLow-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique for large models. Instead of updating all model weights, LoRA freezes the pretrained model and injects small trainable low-rank matrices into selected layers. This reduces computational cost, memory usage, and the number of trainable parameters while retaining strong performance. [1] The model update is represented as a low-rank decomposition using matrices A and B, so only these matrices are trained during fine-tuning. Computational complexity is reduced because the original model remains unchanged. [2] LoRA is widely used for parameter-efficient fine-tuning of LLMs and has been combined with quantization methods to further reduce storage and memory requirements during training. [1]​[3] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2412.20772v2.pdf "2412.20772v2.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_02/2403.05887v2.pdf "2403.05887v2.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_07/2412.04468v2.pdf "2412.04468v2.pdf"

Evaluating reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) typiEvaluating reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) typically involves testing how well they solve problems requiring logic, inference, planning, and multi-step thinking rather than simple recall. Common evaluation approaches include: - General reasoning benchmarks such as MMLU, which assesses knowledge and reasoning across diverse subjects. [1] - Mathematical reasoning benchmarks such as GSM8K, which focus on multi-step problem solving. [1] - Logical reasoning evaluations that aim for systematic assessment of reasoning ability. [2] - Long-context reasoning tests that measure whether models can retrieve and connect information scattered across very large contexts. Research shows effective context limits may be shorter than supported context lengths for many models. [3] - Domain-specific reasoning benchmarks, such as CodeMMLU for code understanding and reasoning. Results indicate even state-of-the-art models still struggle on some reasoning-intensive code tasks. [4] Research also highlights limitations, including reasoning breakdowns on simple tasks and weaknesses in mathematical reasoning, underscoring the need for robust evaluation methods. **Multimodal Learning: Combining Vision and Language** Multimodal lea**Multimodal Learning: Combining Vision and Language** Multimodal learning integrates visual data (images, videos) with language data (text) to build models that can understand and reason across both modalities. Vision-language models align visual and textual information in a shared representation space, enabling tasks such as image captioning, visual question answering, image retrieval, and visual reasoning. [1] Modern multimodal large language models typically use: - A vision encoder to extract visual features. - A projection/alignment module to map visual features into the language model's embedding space. - A language model that processes both visual and text tokens and generates responses. [2]​[3] Examples include CLIP for vision-language alignment and newer multimodal LLMs that combine visual understanding with language reasoning. 36long context understanding and attention mechanisms16:55:5712.84.06.91.911640yes
**Long Context Understanding and Attention Mechanisms** Attention mec**Long Context Understanding and Attention Mechanisms** Attention mechanisms are the core of Transformer models. For each token, the model creates query, key, and value vectors, then computes attention scores to determine which other tokens are most relevant. This enables contextual understanding by dynamically weighting information across a sequence. [1]​[2] For long-context understanding, models must effectively use information spread across very large inputs. Research notes that longer contexts can support tasks such as legal document analysis, academic research, and complex retrieval across many sources. However, performance can degrade in the middle of long contexts, and Transformer attention has quadratic time and memory complexity with sequence length. [3]​[4] Common techniques for efficient long-context processing include Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), Sliding Window Attention (SWA), and memory-efficient caching methods. 37knowledge distillation for smaller language models16:56:1111.63.66.02.011100yes
Knowledge distillation is a model-compression technique where a large,Knowledge distillation is a model-compression technique where a large, powerful “teacher” model transfers knowledge to a smaller “student” model. The student learns to mimic the teacher’s behavior, allowing it to achieve strong performance with fewer parameters and lower compute requirements. [1] For language models, the student is often trained using the teacher’s output probabilities (“soft labels”) rather than only the correct answers. This helps the student learn richer patterns and generalize better. [2]​[1] Benefits: - Smaller model size and faster inference. [2] - Reduced computational and memory requirements. [2] - Performance that can approach, and sometimes exceed, the teacher under certain conditions. [2] Examples include distilling large models such as BERT into compact variants like TinyBERT. 38quantization techniques for efficient model inference16:56:2411.94.35.62.011713yes
**Quantization Techniques for Efficient Model Inference** Quantizatio**Quantization Techniques for Efficient Model Inference** Quantization reduces model precision (for example, FP32 to INT8, INT4, or lower) to decrease memory usage and improve inference speed. Research cited in the knowledge base reports that 8-bit and 4-bit quantization can maintain performance close to full-precision models while reducing computational costs. [1] Common techniques include: - Uniform quantization, which maps values into evenly spaced discrete levels. [2] - Non-uniform quantization such as NF4 (NormalFloat 4-bit), which assumes normally distributed weights and can improve low-bit accuracy. [2] - Mixed-precision quantization, where different model components use different precisions (for example W8A8 or W4A16). [3] - Post-training quantization (PTQ), converting a trained model to lower precision after training. An example uses 8-bit asymmetric per-channel quantization with no observed accuracy loss. [4] Quantization can significantly reduce inference latency while preserving accuracy. 312tool use and planning in autonomous AI agents16:57:1411.33.75.91.711515yes
Tool use and planning are core capabilities of autonomous AI agents. Tool use and planning are core capabilities of autonomous AI agents. Tool use allows agents to interact with external systems such as databases, search engines, APIs, code interpreters, and custom software. Agents can select appropriate tools, execute actions, observe results, and refine their behavior based on feedback. Some agent frameworks follow a thought–action–observation cycle, repeatedly choosing tools and evaluating outputs until a task is completed. [1] Planning enables agents to decompose complex goals into smaller tasks, coordinate actions, and adapt to changing conditions. Research on LLM agents highlights reasoning, planning, memory, and tool use as key capabilities. Techniques such as Chain-of-Thought and ReAct support step-by-step reasoning and tool-assisted decision making. [2] Recent work also explores multi-agent systems, context-aware planning, memory-based agents, and agents that can create or refine their own tools. 313improving factual accuracy through retrieval16:57:2610.64.04.91.711800yes
Retrieval can improve factual accuracy by grounding responses in relevRetrieval can improve factual accuracy by grounding responses in relevant external information instead of relying only on a model’s internal knowledge. Key techniques include: - Retrieving the most relevant documents or passages before generation to provide evidence-based context. [1] - Using similarity search and semantic retrieval to find highly relevant information for a query. [2] - Applying reranking after initial retrieval so the most relevant sources are prioritized, improving output quality and accuracy. [3]​[4] - Limiting irrelevant retrieved content, since excessive or noisy context can reduce accuracy. Studies found that retrieving a small number of highly relevant passages often performs better than retrieving many passages. [5]​[6] This approach is commonly known as Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). [7] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_08/2408.05109v4.pdf "2408.05109v4.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_03/2410.11843v5.pdf "2410.11843v5.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2409.15076v1.pdf "2409.15076v1.pdf" [4]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_07/2410.23841v2.pdf "2410.23841v2.pdf" [5]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_08/2412.02868v2.pdf "2412.02868v2.pdf" [6]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_08/2410.21591v2.pdf "2410.21591v2.pdf" [7]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_05/2411.00816v2.pdf "2411.00816v2.pdf"
314mixture of experts architectures and routing16:57:3812.53.96.42.211570yes
**Mixture of Experts (MoE)** is a model architecture that combines mul**Mixture of Experts (MoE)** is a model architecture that combines multiple specialized neural networks called experts. Instead of activating all experts for every input, a routing mechanism selects only the most relevant experts, allowing the model to scale to many parameters while keeping computation relatively low. [1] **Routing** is the process that determines which experts handle a given input. A router computes scores between the input and available experts, then assigns higher weights to the most relevant experts. In sparse MoE systems, only a subset of experts is activated for each input. [1] Common routing approaches include: - Linear routers based on similarity scores between token representations and expert embeddings. [1] - Cosine routers, introduced to mitigate expert dominance and representation collapse. [1] - Noisy top-k gating, which selects the top-ranked experts for processing. [2] Modern LLMs such as Mixtral and DBRX use MoE architectures to increase capacity efficiently. [3]​[4] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2405.14131v3.pdf "2405.14131v3.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_09/2408.04307v3.pdf "2408.04307v3.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_05/2406.02969v2.pdf "2406.02969v2.pdf" [4]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06412v2.pdf "2412.06412v2.pdf"
315safety alignment and robustness of language models16:57:5110.63.84.72.111523yes
Safety alignment aims to make language models behave consistently withSafety alignment aims to make language models behave consistently with human values, ethical norms, and intended goals. Common techniques include supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), and instruction tuning. Despite these measures, research shows that aligned models can remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks that bypass safety mechanisms. [1] Robustness refers to a model’s ability to remain reliable under challenging conditions, including adversarial prompts, data contamination, prompt variations, and distribution shifts. Studies highlight vulnerabilities such as jailbreak attacks, adversarial inputs, privacy risks, and sensitivity to prompt structure, demonstrating the need for stronger robustness evaluation and defenses. [1]​[2]​[3] Current research focuses on improved safety alignment, adversarial robustness testing, red teaming, and defenses against jailbreak attacks to build more reliable language models. 318instruction tuning and preference optimization16:58:2710.63.75.31.611528yes
**Instruction tuning** is a fine-tuning stage where a pretrained model**Instruction tuning** is a fine-tuning stage where a pretrained model is trained on instruction–response examples so it learns to follow human instructions and perform downstream tasks more effectively. Instruction-tuned models are designed to produce coherent outputs from well-defined instructions and examples. [1]​[2] **Preference optimization** is a model-alignment approach that uses preference data (comparisons between preferred and rejected responses) to better align model behavior with human preferences. Methods such as Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) are widely used as an alternative to traditional RLHF and are employed after supervised fine-tuning. [3]​[4] A common modern pipeline is: 1. Pretraining 2. Instruction tuning (SFT) 3. Preference optimization (e.g., DPO) for alignment with human preferences. 319inference time scaling and chain of thought reasoning16:58:3911.43.95.91.611718yes
Inference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s performance Inference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s performance by using more computation during inference (when answering a question), rather than only making the model larger during training. One example is increasing the length of the reasoning process or generating multiple candidate solutions. Research notes that OpenAI’s o1 series introduced inference-time scaling by increasing the length of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, leading to gains in math, coding, and scientific reasoning tasks. [1] Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning is a prompting technique that encourages a model to solve problems through intermediate reasoning steps rather than jumping directly to an answer. It has been shown to elicit reasoning in large language models and can improve performance on complex tasks by breaking them into smaller steps. [2]​[3] The effectiveness of CoT varies by task: some studies report improvements in reasoning tasks, while others find that CoT can reduce performance in certain coding benchmarks. 320data quality and deduplication for model pretraining16:58:5211.13.54.92.611602yes
For model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical becFor model pretraining, data quality and deduplication are critical because duplicate or low-quality data can reduce training efficiency, increase memorization, and harm generalization. Key practices include: - Remove low-quality documents (e.g., very short, noisy, corrupted, or irrelevant content). SlimPajama filters documents with insufficient meaningful content before training. [1] - Perform exact and near-duplicate removal across all data sources, not just within individual datasets. Cross-source duplicates are a significant issue in large web corpora. [1] - Use MinHash and Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH) to identify near-duplicates efficiently at scale. [1] - Apply similarity measures such as Jaccard similarity with defined thresholds to remove redundant content. [1]​[2] - Use quality filters and language identification to retain high-quality, relevant documents. [1]​[3] Benefits: - Improved training efficiency. - Reduced memorization and overfitting. - More diverse training data. - Better downstream model performance. 41retrieval augmented generation for large language models16:59:3010.34.94.01.311200yes
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances LargRetrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances Large Language Models (LLMs) by retrieving relevant external information and providing it to the model before generating a response. This helps reduce hallucinations and improves accuracy on knowledge-intensive tasks. The concept was formalized in the paper “Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP Tasks” by Lewis et al. (2020). [1] A typical RAG pipeline: • Convert documents into searchable representations. • Retrieve relevant content for a user query. • Supply the retrieved content as context to the LLM. • Generate an answer grounded in that retrieved information. RAG is widely used in domains requiring up-to-date or specialized knowledge, including healthcare and code generation. [2]​[3] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_05/2411.00816v2.pdf "2411.00816v2.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_08/2412.02868v2.pdf "2412.02868v2.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_08/2412.06947v3.pdf "2412.06947v3.pdf"
42reducing hallucinations in language model responses16:59:4111.33.55.12.612051yes
Reducing hallucinations in language model responses typically involvesReducing hallucinations in language model responses typically involves grounding, prompting, and model-improvement techniques. • Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG): Incorporating external knowledge during generation helps reduce errors caused by outdated information or unsupported claims. RAG dynamically retrieves relevant sources and injects them into prompts. [1]​[2] • Structured reasoning: Step-by-step instructions and Chain-of-Thought-style prompting have been reported to improve task performance and reduce hallucinations in some settings. [1]​[3] • Grounding and alignment: Research highlights grounding responses in observed data or retrieved evidence as a way to reduce hallucinations. [4]​[5] • Abstention and uncertainty handling: Some work focuses on identifying knowledge gaps and abstaining when confidence is insufficient rather than generating unsupported answers. [6] Note that some research suggests hallucinations may remain an inherent limitation of current autoregressive language models, making mitigation more realistic than complete elimination. 43efficient fine tuning with low rank adaptation16:59:5310.03.74.71.511312yes
Efficient fine-tuning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-eEfficient fine-tuning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient method for adapting large pre-trained models. Instead of updating all model weights, LoRA freezes the original weights and injects small trainable low-rank matrices into selected layers. During training, only these low-rank matrices are updated. [1] Benefits include: - Much fewer trainable parameters, reducing computational cost and memory usage. [2] - Avoiding full-model fine-tuning, which can be impractical for large language models. [2] - Helping preserve the model’s original knowledge and reducing catastrophic forgetting. [2] LoRA is widely used for LLMs and other foundation models because it enables efficient adaptation while maintaining strong performance. 45multimodal learning combining vision and language17:00:1710.63.85.51.313855yes
**Multimodal learning combining vision and language** is an AI approac**Multimodal learning combining vision and language** is an AI approach that learns from both visual data (images, videos) and language data (text) to build a shared understanding across modalities. Vision-language learning is used for understanding and generating language that describes visual experiences. [1] Common capabilities include: - Image captioning (describing images in text) - Visual question answering - Text-to-image generation - Visual recognition and scene understanding - Vision-language navigation and instruction following [2]​[3] Modern multimodal models align visual representations with language models in a shared semantic space, enabling them to process images and text together and perform both understanding and generation tasks. 46long context understanding and attention mechanisms17:00:2911.84.25.81.811651yes
**Long Context Understanding** Long context understanding is a model'**Long Context Understanding** Long context understanding is a model's ability to process, retain, and reason over information spread across very large inputs, such as long documents, books, conversations, or collections of files. Longer context windows can help with tasks like legal document analysis, research, and information retrieval across many sources. However, model performance may decline as context grows, especially when important information is buried in the middle of the input. [1] **Attention Mechanisms** Attention is the core mechanism used by Transformers to determine which parts of the input are most relevant for each token. Each token is represented by query, key, and value vectors. The model computes attention scores and uses them to weight information from other tokens, enabling contextual understanding and long-range dependency modeling. [2]​[3] For long contexts, techniques such as Grouped-Query Attention (GQA) and Sliding Window Attention (SWA) are used to reduce memory and computation costs while preserving the ability to model long-range dependencies. 47knowledge distillation for smaller language models17:00:4212.04.26.21.711211yes
**Knowledge Distillation for Smaller Language Models** Knowledge dist**Knowledge Distillation for Smaller Language Models** Knowledge distillation is a model compression technique where a large, powerful model (the "teacher") transfers its knowledge to a smaller model (the "student"). The student is trained to mimic the teacher's behavior, allowing it to achieve performance closer to the larger model while using fewer parameters and less compute. [1] Key benefits include: - Reduced model size and computational requirements. [2] - Faster inference and deployment on resource-constrained devices. [2] - Performance that can approach, and sometimes exceed, the teacher model under certain conditions. [2] For language models, knowledge distillation is commonly combined with techniques such as pruning and data selection to create compact, efficient LLMs. [3] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_04/2407.12296v2.pdf "2407.12296v2.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_03/2411.10703v2.pdf "2411.10703v2.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_07/2412.04468v2.pdf "2412.04468v2.pdf"
48quantization techniques for efficient model inference17:00:5510.84.15.51.211194yes
Quantization reduces model precision (for example, FP32 to INT8, 8-bitQuantization reduces model precision (for example, FP32 to INT8, 8-bit, or 4-bit weights) to lower memory use and accelerate inference while often preserving accuracy. Research in the knowledge base highlights: - INT8 quantization can significantly reduce inference latency with minimal accuracy impact. Larger models often benefit more from quantization than smaller ones. [1] - 8-bit quantized LLM inference can achieve predictive performance similar to full-precision inference while reducing GPU memory and speeding execution. [2] - Popular LLM quantization methods include AWQ and GPTQ. AWQ has also been applied to vision-language models. [3] - Mixed-precision approaches such as W8A8 and W4A16 are used to accelerate different inference stages and improve throughput. 49reinforcement learning from human feedback17:01:0711.44.84.71.911233yes
**Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF)** is a method used**Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF)** is a method used to align AI models with human preferences and values. It is widely used to make language models more helpful and less harmful. [1] A typical RLHF pipeline includes: - Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), where demonstration data teaches desired behaviors. - A reinforcement learning stage that uses human preference data, comparing different responses to the same prompt and learning which responses humans prefer. [1] RLHF has become a primary approach for aligning large language models and has contributed significantly to the success of systems such as ChatGPT and GPT-4. [1]​[2] Research also notes limitations and open challenges, including potential bias, reduced output diversity, and difficulties in obtaining high-quality human feedback. Tool use and planning are core capabilities of autonomous AI agents. • Tool use enables agents to interact with external resources such as databases, search engines, APIs, code execution environments, and specialized software. ReAct-style agents refine outputs based on previous tool results and can use external tools to solve tasks. [1] • Planning allows agents to break complex goals into smaller actions, select appropriate tools, and execute multi-step workflows. [2] • Memory supports planning by storing and retrieving relevant information. Multi-level memory architectures help agents maintain context and improve decision-making across long tasks. [2] • Recent research explores agents that can create, refine, and coordinate tools autonomously, extending beyond simple tool usage. The retrieved sources indicate several benefits: - Retrieval-based methods select information or examples most relevant to a query, which can improve accuracy and efficiency. 414mixture of experts architectures and routing17:02:0811.74.15.12.511723yes
Mixture of Experts (MoE) is an architecture that combines multiple speMixture of Experts (MoE) is an architecture that combines multiple specialized neural networks (“experts”) with a routing mechanism that decides which experts should process each input token. Instead of activating all parameters, the router selects the most relevant experts, improving model capacity while keeping computation relatively efficient. [1] Routing is the core mechanism in MoE: - The router computes similarity scores between an input token and available experts. - It assigns higher weights to more relevant experts. - Sparse MoE variants route each token to only a subset of experts (often top-k experts), enabling much larger models with nearly constant computational cost. [1] Common routing approaches include: - Linear routers based on inner products between token representations and expert embeddings. [1] - Cosine routers, introduced to help mitigate representation collapse, where a small number of experts dominate usage. [1] - Noisy top-k softmax gating networks that select top-ranked experts for activation. [2] Modern MoE systems such as Mixtral and DeepSeek use these sparse routing principles to scale model capacity efficiently. [3]​[4] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2405.14131v3.pdf "2405.14131v3.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_09/2408.04307v3.pdf "2408.04307v3.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf" [4]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06412v2.pdf "2412.06412v2.pdf"
415safety alignment and robustness of language models17:02:2011.34.54.72.111276yes
**Safety Alignment and Robustness of Language Models** Safety alignme**Safety Alignment and Robustness of Language Models** Safety alignment aims to make language models helpful and less harmful, commonly using techniques such as supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF). However, research shows that aligned models can still be vulnerable to jailbreak attacks that bypass safety mechanisms through carefully crafted prompts. [1] Robustness refers to a model’s ability to maintain safe and reliable behavior under adversarial inputs and changing conditions. Studies have found: - Advanced reasoning capabilities can expose safety vulnerabilities and enable successful jailbreak attacks. [1] - Existing alignment methods may suffer from issues such as “gradient entanglement,” which can lead to safety alignment failures where undesirable responses are not sufficiently suppressed. [2] - More comprehensive safety alignment frameworks and stronger defenses are needed to improve reliability without degrading model performance. 418instruction tuning and preference optimization17:02:5811.75.15.01.511645yes
Instruction tuning is a post-training process where a model is trainedInstruction tuning is a post-training process where a model is trained on instruction-response pairs to improve its ability to follow user requests and perform downstream tasks. It is commonly done using supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on curated datasets of prompts and desired answers. [1]​[2] Preference optimization is a later alignment stage that uses preference data—comparisons between preferred and rejected responses—to make model outputs better match human preferences. Methods such as Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and SimPO are examples of preference optimization approaches used after supervised fine-tuning. [3]​[4]​[5] In a typical alignment pipeline: - Instruction tuning teaches the model what to do. - Preference optimization teaches the model which responses are preferred by humans. 419inference time scaling and chain of thought reasoning17:03:1011.04.15.51.411696yes
Inference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s reasoning atInference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s reasoning at test time by allocating more computation during inference, rather than only increasing model size or training data. One approach is to increase the length or depth of reasoning steps. Research cited in the knowledge base notes that OpenAI’s o1 models introduced inference-time scaling by increasing the length of the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning process, leading to improvements in mathematics, coding, and scientific reasoning tasks. [1] Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning is a prompting technique that encourages a model to break a problem into sequential, step-by-step reasoning steps before producing an answer. It has been shown to improve performance on many reasoning tasks, especially those requiring multiple layers of reasoning and decision-making. [2]​[3] The effectiveness of CoT varies by task and model; some studies report strong gains, while others find limited or even negative effects in certain domains. 420data quality and deduplication for model pretraining17:03:2211.03.65.02.411151yes
Data quality and deduplication are critical for model pretraining becaData quality and deduplication are critical for model pretraining because they improve training efficiency, reduce memorization, and increase dataset diversity. Studies cited in the knowledge base describe several common practices: - Remove low-quality content, such as very short or information-poor documents. [1] - Apply exact and near-duplicate removal across and within data sources using techniques such as MinHash, Locality Sensitive Hashing (LSH), and Jaccard similarity thresholds. [1] - Use quality filters and classifiers to retain higher-quality documents. [1] - Deduplicate to reduce overfitting and improve diversity in training data. [2] - Deduplication can substantially shrink datasets while preserving quality; one example removed nearly 50% of bytes from a large corpus. [1] These steps help create cleaner, more representative pretraining datasets. 51retrieval augmented generation for large language models17:04:0111.04.45.21.511645yes
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances LargRetrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances Large Language Models (LLMs) by retrieving relevant external information and supplying it to the model before generating a response. This helps improve factual accuracy, reduce hallucinations, and incorporate up-to-date or domain-specific knowledge. [1] A foundational RAG approach for knowledge-intensive NLP tasks was introduced by Lewis et al. in 2020. [2]​[3] Typical RAG workflow: - Retrieve relevant documents from a knowledge source. - Rank or filter the retrieved content. - Provide the retrieved context to the LLM. - Generate an answer grounded in that context. [4] RAG is used in areas such as healthcare, scientific documentation, and domain-specific code generation to improve reliability and access specialized knowledge. 53efficient fine tuning with low rank adaptation17:04:2410.73.55.31.911313yes
**Efficient Fine-Tuning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)** LoRA is a p**Efficient Fine-Tuning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)** LoRA is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique for large language models (LLMs). Instead of updating all model parameters, it freezes the pretrained weights and adds small trainable low-rank matrices to selected layers. [1] The approach modifies a weight matrix W by adding a low-rank update ABᵀ, where only matrices A and B are trained while the original weights remain fixed. Because the rank is much smaller than the full matrix dimensions, the number of trainable parameters is greatly reduced. [1]​[2] Benefits include: - Lower computational and memory requirements compared to full fine-tuning. [1] - Reduced number of trainable parameters. [1] - Better preservation of the pretrained model’s knowledge, helping mitigate catastrophic forgetting. [1] LoRA is widely used to adapt LLMs efficiently for downstream tasks. 56long context understanding and attention mechanisms17:05:0010.43.45.01.911538yes
**Long Context Understanding** Long context understanding is a model’**Long Context Understanding** Long context understanding is a model’s ability to process, remember, and reason over large amounts of information distributed across long inputs, such as lengthy documents, books, conversations, or multiple data sources. Longer contexts can improve tasks like document retrieval, research, and complex reasoning, but models may struggle to retrieve information consistently across very large contexts. [1] **Attention Mechanisms** Attention mechanisms help models determine which parts of the input are most relevant. In Transformers, each token generates query, key, and value representations, and attention weights are computed to build contextual understanding from related tokens. Multi-head attention allows the model to capture different relationships simultaneously. [2]​[3] For long contexts, techniques such as Grouped-Query Attention (GQA) and Sliding Window Attention (SWA) improve efficiency while preserving long-range dependencies. 57knowledge distillation for smaller language models17:05:1210.34.04.71.611222yes
Knowledge distillation is a model compression technique where a large,Knowledge distillation is a model compression technique where a large, powerful “teacher” model transfers knowledge to a smaller “student” model. The student is trained to mimic the teacher’s behavior, allowing it to achieve strong performance with fewer parameters and lower computational cost. [1]​[2] For language models, distillation is commonly used to create compact versions of large models (for example, compressing BERT into smaller variants). The student learns not only from ground-truth labels but also from the teacher’s predictions and representations through an additional distillation loss. [1] Benefits include: - Reduced model size and memory usage. - Faster inference. - Lower deployment costs. - Performance that can approach, and occasionally exceed, the teacher in some settings. 58quantization techniques for efficient model inference17:05:2310.93.75.21.911718yes
Quantization reduces model precision (for example, from FP32 to INT8, Quantization reduces model precision (for example, from FP32 to INT8, 8-bit, or 4-bit) to lower memory usage and improve inference speed while maintaining acceptable accuracy. Common techniques include: - Post-Training Quantization (PTQ): Quantizes a trained model without retraining. An example uses 8-bit asymmetric per-channel quantization with no observed accuracy loss. [1] - Integer Quantization (INT8): Converts weights and activations to 8-bit integers, reducing latency and memory requirements. Studies report substantial latency reductions with minimal accuracy impact. [2] - Low-Bit Quantization (4-bit/NF4): Uses 4-bit representations such as NormalFloat Quantization (NF4), designed for efficient large-model deployment. [3] - Mixed-Precision Quantization: Applies different precisions to different model components for a balance of speed and accuracy. [4] Research also shows 8-bit quantized models can achieve performance similar to full-precision models while saving GPU memory and accelerating inference. 59reinforcement learning from human feedback17:05:3511.14.15.12.01957yes
Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is a technique used Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is a technique used to align AI models with human preferences. It typically involves two stages: - Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), where demonstration data teaches the model desired behaviors. - A reinforcement learning stage that uses human preference data (comparisons between responses) to help the model distinguish preferred from rejected outputs. [1] RLHF has become a primary method for making language models more helpful and less harmful. [1] Recent research also notes limitations, including challenges with preference optimization and potential reductions in output diversity when feedback data is homogeneous. **Tool Use and Planning in Autonomous AI Agents** Autonomous AI agent**Tool Use and Planning in Autonomous AI Agents** Autonomous AI agents combine reasoning, planning, and tool usage to achieve goals with limited human intervention. Tool use allows agents to interact with external systems such as databases, search engines, APIs, code execution environments, and specialized applications. ReAct-style approaches enable agents to iteratively reason, take actions with tools, observe results, and refine subsequent actions. [4] Emerging work also explores agents that can create, refine, and coordinate tools autonomously, extending their problem-solving capabilities. 514mixture of experts architectures and routing17:06:4011.74.15.22.411527yes
**Mixture of Experts (MoE) Architectures** MoE is a neural network de**Mixture of Experts (MoE) Architectures** MoE is a neural network design that combines multiple specialized sub-networks called experts. Instead of activating every expert for every input, only a small subset is used, allowing very large models with relatively low computation. MoE was introduced to combine specialized models and later evolved into sparse MoE systems used in modern AI models. [1] **Routing (Gating)** Routing is the mechanism that decides which experts should process a given input. The router computes scores between the input and available experts, then assigns higher weights to the most relevant experts. Sparse MoE systems typically route each input to only a subset of experts, increasing scalability while keeping computational costs nearly constant. [1] Modern MoE layers commonly consist of: - Multiple expert feed-forward networks. - A trainable gating (routing) network that selectively activates experts. [2] Examples of routing approaches mentioned in the literature include learned routing layers, noisy top-k softmax gating, linear routers, and cosine routers. [3]​[2]​[1] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2405.14131v3.pdf "2405.14131v3.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_09/2408.04307v3.pdf "2408.04307v3.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_05/2406.02969v2.pdf "2406.02969v2.pdf"
515safety alignment and robustness of language models17:06:5310.84.05.31.611444yes
Safety alignment aims to make language models behave in ways that are Safety alignment aims to make language models behave in ways that are helpful and less harmful, often using techniques such as Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) and preference optimization. Research highlights that alignment methods can have limitations; for example, margin-based preference optimization may unintentionally increase the probability of undesirable responses or decrease preferred responses due to a phenomenon called “gradient entanglement.” [1] Robustness refers to a model’s ability to remain reliable under adversarial inputs, jailbreak attempts, and distribution shifts. Studies report vulnerabilities to jailbreak attacks, including transferable adversarial attacks and reasoning-based attacks that can bypass safety mechanisms, emphasizing the need for stronger defenses and evaluation methods. [2]​[3] Recent work also explores defenses such as enhanced safety alignment, inference-time alignment, and jailbreak mitigation techniques to improve model safety while preserving utility. **Code Generation and Automated Software Engineering** Code generatio**Code Generation and Automated Software Engineering** Code generation uses AI models to create software from natural-language requirements, existing code, or specifications. Recent research shows LLMs are being applied to code generation, code completion, debugging, repair, and software development workflows. [1]​[2] Automated software engineering extends beyond generating code. It includes: - Automated code generation and completion. [1] - Automated program repair and bug fixing. [3] - Agent-based software engineering systems such as SWE-agent. [4] - Iterative feedback, testing, verification, and code review workflows. [5]​[6] Research also highlights limitations, including hallucinations, bias, and the need for stronger code understanding and reasoning, not just code generation. 517embedding models for semantic document retrieval17:07:1710.74.15.21.311087yes
For semantic document retrieval, the retrieved sources highlight denseFor semantic document retrieval, the retrieved sources highlight dense embedding models as a strong approach because they encode text into vectors that capture semantic meaning and enable similarity search. [1] The sources also note that retrieval quality can be improved by combining embedding-based retrieval with a reranking stage using cross-encoders, which evaluate the query and retrieved documents together for better relevance ranking. [2] A typical semantic retrieval pipeline embeds documents, stores embeddings in a vector database, embeds the query, and returns the most similar documents using vector similarity (such as cosine similarity). 518instruction tuning and preference optimization17:07:2810.93.95.21.811546yes
Instruction tuning is a post-training process where a model is trainedInstruction tuning is a post-training process where a model is trained on instruction-response examples to improve its ability to follow human commands and perform downstream tasks. It is commonly implemented through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) using datasets of instructions and desired responses. [1]​[2] Preference optimization is a model alignment technique that uses preference data—comparisons between preferred and rejected responses—to teach a model which outputs are more desirable. It is often used after supervised fine-tuning as part of RLHF-style alignment pipelines, with methods such as Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and SimPO. [3]​[4] In practice, instruction tuning teaches a model how to follow instructions, while preference optimization aligns its behavior with human preferences for response quality and helpfulness. [3]​[5] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2408.11878v2.pdf "2408.11878v2.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_07/2407.18271v4.pdf "2407.18271v4.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_04/2410.13828v2.pdf "2410.13828v2.pdf" [4]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_05/2411.00816v2.pdf "2411.00816v2.pdf" [5]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf"
519inference time scaling and chain of thought reasoning17:07:4010.93.74.72.411773yes
Inference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s performance Inference-time scaling is the idea of improving a model’s performance by using more computation during inference, rather than only making the model larger during training. One approach is to increase the length or depth of reasoning steps at inference time, which has been reported to improve performance on tasks such as mathematics, coding, and scientific reasoning. [1] Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning is a prompting technique that encourages a model to break a problem into intermediate steps and reason sequentially. Research describes CoT as improving reasoning and generalization on complex tasks by decomposing problems into smaller steps. [2]​[3] The relationship is that inference-time scaling can be achieved by allocating more compute to CoT reasoning, for example by generating longer reasoning traces or exploring multiple reasoning paths before producing an answer. [1]​[2] CoT is not universally beneficial; some studies report gains on reasoning tasks, while others find limited or negative effects in certain coding benchmarks or simpler factual tasks. [4]​[5] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2412.06845v4.pdf "2412.06845v4.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_01/2410.05459v2.pdf "2410.05459v2.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2407.02511v2.pdf "2407.02511v2.pdf" [4]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_06/2408.08704v2.pdf "2408.08704v2.pdf" [5]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_09/2410.01999v4.pdf "2410.01999v4.pdf"
520data quality and deduplication for model pretraining17:07:5210.73.94.62.211529yes
**Data Quality and Deduplication for Model Pretraining** High-quality**Data Quality and Deduplication for Model Pretraining** High-quality pretraining data is critical because model performance depends heavily on the quality of the underlying corpus. Research in the knowledge base highlights that stronger data quality can significantly influence downstream performance, sometimes rivaling model scale improvements. [1] Common data-quality practices include: - Filtering low-quality content and retaining only high-confidence samples. - Language identification and filtering to keep data aligned with training goals. [2] - Length and quality filtering to remove problematic content such as noisy text, URLs, code snippets, or malformed records. [2] Deduplication is used to: - Remove duplicate entries and increase dataset diversity. [2] - Reduce overfitting caused by repeated examples. [2] - Prevent data leakage between pretraining and downstream evaluation datasets by removing overlapping records. [3] One example removed duplicate records using canonical representations and eliminated overlaps with benchmark datasets to create a “high-quality and leak-free dataset.” [3] [1]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_09/2410.01999v4.pdf "2410.01999v4.pdf" [2]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_07/2410.15316v3.pdf "2410.15316v3.pdf" [3]: https://m365cpi88726844.sharepoint.com/sites/Contoso-Team-site/Shared%20Documents/arxiv/batch_09/2404.16880v3.pdf "2404.16880v3.pdf"

522Which model extends Mistral from 32 to 36 transformer blocks? Describe its three pre-training phases, the context lengths in the first two phases, and the purpose of the final phase. Cite the supporting sections.17:08:1613.03.86.72.411335yes
The model is Moxin 7B. In its architecture section, the authors state The model is Moxin 7B. In its architecture section, the authors state that they “extend the Mistral model architecture” and that “the original Mistral model comprises 32 blocks” while the extended model “uses 36 transformer blocks.” This depth extension is reported as improving learning capacity and downstream-task performance. [1] Its pre-training is described as three phases: - Phase 1 – Base Pre-training: trained with a fixed context length of 2,000 tokens to build foundational language-modeling abilities, including syntax and basic semantic patterns. [1] - Phase 2 – Extended Context Pre-training: context length increased to 4,000 tokens so the model can learn longer dependencies and richer context. [1] - Phase 3 – Capability Enhancement: adds capability-specific enhancement data (including high-quality benchmark-related training data) to improve model capabilities; the paper includes ablations comparing Phases 1–2 against all three phases to measure the benefit of this enhancement data. [1] Supporting sections: - Section 3.1 “Model Architecture” (32→36 transformer blocks). [1] - Section 3.4 “Training Configuration” (the three pre-training phases and context lengths).
